[[characters/troilus|TROILUS]] AND [[characters/cressida|CRESSIDA]]
[[characters/troilus|Troilus]] and [[characters/cressida|Cressida]], first heard of in a Stationers’ Register entry of 7 February 1603, was probably written within the previous eighteen months. This entry did not result in publication; the play was re-entered on 28 January 1609, and a quarto appeared during that year. The version printed in the 1623 Folio adds a Prologue, and has many variations in dialogue. It includes the epilogue spoken by [[characters/pandarus|Pandarus]] (which we print as an Additional Passage), but certain features of the text suggest that it does so by accident, and that the epilogue had been marked for omission. Our text is based in substance on the Folio in the belief that this represents the play in its later, revised form.
The story of the siege of Troy was the main subject of one of the greatest surviving works of classical literature, Homer’s Iliad, probably Shakespeare read George Chapman’s 1598 [[forms/translation|translation]] of Books 1―2 and 7―11. The story also figures prominently in Virgil’s Aeneid and Ovid’s Metamorphoses, both of which Shakespeare knew [[motifs/well|well]]. The war between Greece and Troy had been provoked by the abduction of the Grecian [[characters/helen|Helen]] (better, if confusingly, known as [[characters/helen|Helen]] of Troy) by the Trojan [[archetypes/hero|hero]] [[settings/paris|Paris]], son of [[archetypes/king|King]] Priam. Shakespeare’s play opens when the Greek forces, led by Menelaus’ brother [[characters/agamemnon|Agamemnon]], have already been besieging Troy for seven years. Shakespeare concentrates on the opposition between the Greek [[archetypes/hero|hero]] [[characters/achilles|Achilles]] and the Trojan [[characters/hector|Hector]]. In the Folio, [[characters/troilus|Troilus]] and [[characters/cressida|Cressida]] is printed among the tragedies; if there is a [[archetypes/tragic-hero|tragic hero]], it is [[characters/hector|Hector]].
Shakespeare also shows how the war caused by one [[motifs/love|love]] affair destroys another. The stories of the [[motifs/love|love]] between the Trojan [[characters/troilus|Troilus]] and the Grecian [[characters/cressida|Cressida]], encouraged by her uncle [[characters/pandarus|Pandarus]], and of [[characters/cressida|Cressida]]’s desertion of [[characters/troilus|Troilus]] for the Greek Diomedes, are medieval additions to the heroic narrative. Chaucer’s long poem [[characters/troilus|Troilus]] and Criseyde was already a classic, and Shakespeare would also have known Robert Henryson’s continuation, The Testament of Cresseid, in which [[characters/cressida|Cressida]], deserted by Diomedes, dwindles into a leprous beggar.
[[characters/troilus|Troilus]] and [[characters/cressida|Cressida]] is a demanding play, Shakespeare’s third longest, highly philosophical in tone and with an exceptionally learned vocabulary. Possibly (as has often been conjectured) he wrote it for private performance; the 1603 Stationers’ Register entry says it had been acted by the [[archetypes/king|King]]’s Men, and the original title-page of the 1609 quarto repeats this claim, but while the edition was being printed this title-page was replaced by one that does not mention performance, and an epistle was added claiming that it was ‘a new play, never staled with the stage, never clapper-clawed with the palms of the vulgar’. An adaptation by John Dryden of 1679 was successfully acted from [[motifs/time|time]] to [[motifs/time|time]] for half a century, but the first verified performance of Shakespeare’s play was in [[settings/germany|Germany]] in 1898, and that was heavily adapted. [[characters/troilus|Troilus]] and [[characters/cressida|Cressida]] came into its own in the twentieth century, when its deflation of heroes, its radical questioning of human values (especially in relation to [[motifs/love|love]] and war), and its remorseless examination of the frailty of human aspirations in the face of the destructive powers of [[motifs/time|time]] seemed particularly apposite to modern intellectual and ethical preoccupations.
THE PERSONS OF THE PLAY
PROLOGUE
Trojans
PRIAM, [[archetypes/king|King]] of Troy
CASSANDRA, Priam’s daughter, a prophetess
ANDROMACHE, wife of [[characters/hector|Hector]]
[[characters/pandarus|PANDARUS]], a lord
[[characters/cressida|CRESSIDA]], his niece
CALCHAS, her father, who has joined the Greeks
[[characters/helen|HELEN]], wife of Menelaus, now living with [[settings/paris|Paris]]
ALEXANDER, servant of [[characters/cressida|Cressida]]
Servants of [[characters/troilus|Troilus]], musicians, soldiers, attendants
Greeks
[[characters/agamemnon|AGAMEMNON]], Commander-in-Chief
MENELAUS, his brother
NESTOR
[[characters/ulysses|ULYSSES]]
[[characters/achilles|ACHILLES]]
PATROCLUS, his companion
DIOMEDES
[[characters/ajax|AJAX]]
[[characters/thersites|THERSITES]]
MYRMIDONS, soldiers of [[characters/achilles|Achilles]]
Servants of Diomedes, soldiers

[[characters/troilus|Troilus]] and [[characters/cressida|Cressida]]

Prologue
Enter the Prologue armed
PROLOGUE
In Troy there lies the scene. From isles of Greece
The princes orgulous, their high [[motifs/blood|blood]] chafed,
Have to the port of Athens sent their ships,
Fraught with the ministers and instruments
Of cruel war. Sixty-and-nine, that wore
Their crownets regal, from th‘Athenian bay
Put forth toward Phrygia, and their vow is made
To ransack Troy, within whose strong immures
The ravished [[characters/helen|Helen]], Menelaus’ [[characters/queen|queen]],
With wanton [[settings/paris|Paris]] steeps—and that’s the quarrel.
To Tenedos they come,
And the deep-drawing barques do there disgorge
Their warlike freightage; now on Dardan plains
The fresh and yet unbruisèd Greeks do pitch
Their brave pavilions. Priam’s six-gated [[motifs/city|city]]—
Dardan and Timbria, Helias, Chetas, Troien,
And Antenorides—with massy staples
And corresponsive and full-filling bolts
Spar up the sons of Troy.
Now expectation, tickling skittish spirits
On one and other side, Trojan and Greek,
Sets all on hazard. And hither am I come,
A Prologue armed—but not in confidence
Of author’s pen or actor’s voice, but suited
In like conditions as our argument—
To tell you, fair beholders, that our play
Leaps o’er the vaunt and firstlings of those broils,
Beginning in the middle, starting thence away
To what may be digested in a play.
Like or find fault; do as your pleasures are;
Now, good or bad, ’tis but the chance of war.
Exit

### Atto 1, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/pandarus\|Pandarus]], and [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] armed |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Call here my varlet. I’ll unarm again.<br>Why should I war without the walls of Troy<br>That find such cruel battle here within?<br>Each Trojan that is master of his heart,<br>Let him to Betd—Troitus, alas, hath none. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Will this gear ne’er be mended? |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | The Greeks are strong, and skilful to their strength,<br>Fierce to their skill, and to their fierceness valiant.<br>But I am weaker than a woman’s tear,<br>Tamer than [[motifs/sleep\|sleep]], fonder than ignorance,<br>Less valiant than the virgin in the [[motifs/night\|night]],<br>And skilless as unpractised infancy. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], I have told you enough of this. For my part, I’ll not meddle nor make no farther. He that will have a cake out of the wheat must tarry the grinding. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Have I not tarried? |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Ay, the grinding; but you must tarry the boulting. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Have I not tarried? |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Ay, the boulting; but you must tarry the leavening. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Still have I tarried. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Ay, to the leavening; but here’s yet in the word ‘hereafter’ the kneading, the making of the cake, the heating the oven, and the baking—nay, you must stay the cooling too, or ye may chance burn your lips. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Patience herself, what goddess e‘er she be,<br>Doth lesser blench at suff’rance than I do.<br>At Priam’s royal table do I sit<br>And when fair Cressid comes into my thoughts—<br>So, traitor! ‘When she comes’? When is she thence? |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], she looked yesternight fairer than ever I saw her look, or any woman else. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | I was about to tell thee: when my heart,<br>As wedged with a sigh, would rive in twain,<br>Lest [[characters/hector\|Hector]] or my father should perceive me<br>I have, as when the sun doth light askance,<br>Buried this sigh in wrinkle of a smile.<br>But sorrow that is couched in seeming gladness<br>Is like that mirth fate turns to sudden sadness. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | An her hair were not somewhat darker than [[characters/helen\|Helen]]‘s—[[motifs/well\|well]], go to, there were no more comparison between the women. But, for my part, she is my kinswoman; I would not, as they term it, ‘praise’ her. But I would somebody had heard her talk yesterday, as I did. I will not dispraise your sister Cassandra’s wit, but— |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | O [[characters/pandarus\|Pandarus]]! I tell thee, [[characters/pandarus\|Pandarus]],<br>When I do tell thee ‘There my hopes lie drowned’,<br>Reply not in how many fathoms deep<br>They lie endrenched. I tell thee I am mad<br>In Cressid’s [[motifs/love\|love]]; thou answer’st ‘She is fair’,<br>Pourest in the open ulcer of my heart<br>Her eyes, her hair, her cheek, her gait, her voice;<br>Handlest in thy discourse, O, that her hand,<br>In whose comparison all whites are ink<br>Writing their own reproach, to whose soft seizure<br>The cygnet’s down is harsh, and spirit of sense<br>Hard as the palm of ploughman. This thou tell’st me—<br>As true thou tell‘st me—when I say I [[motifs/love\|love]] her.<br>But saying thus, instead of oil and balm<br>Thou lay’st in every gash that [[motifs/love\|love]] hath given me<br>The knife that made it. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | I speak no more than [[concepts/truth\|truth]]. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Thou dost not speak so much. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | [[concepts/faith\|Faith]], I’ll not meddle in it. Let her be as she is. If she be fair, ’tis the better for her; an she be not, she has the mends in her own hands. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Good [[characters/pandarus\|Pandarus]], how now, [[characters/pandarus\|Pandarus]]! |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | I have had my labour for my travail. Ill thought on of her and ill thought on of you. Gone between and between, but small thanks for my labour. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | What, art thou angry, [[characters/pandarus\|Pandarus]]? What, with me? |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Because she’s kin to me, therefore she’s not so fair as [[characters/helen\|Helen]]. An she were not kin to me, she would be as fair o’ Friday as [[characters/helen\|Helen]] is on [[characters/sunday\|Sunday]]. But what care I? I care not an she were a blackamoor. ’Tis all one to me. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Say I she is not fair? |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | I do not care whether you do or no. She’s a [[archetypes/fool\|fool]] to stay behind her father. Let her to the Greeks—and so I’ll tell her the next [[motifs/time\|time]] I see her. For my part, I’ll meddle nor make no more i’th’ matter. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | [[characters/pandarus\|Pandarus]]— |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Not I. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Sweet [[characters/pandarus\|Pandarus]]— |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Pray you, speak no more to me. I will leave all as I found it. And there an end. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| *(didascalia)* | Alarum |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Peace, you ungracious clamours! Peace, rude sounds!<br>Fools on both sides. [[characters/helen\|Helen]] must needs be fair<br>When with your [[motifs/blood\|blood]] you daily paint her thus.<br>I cannot fight upon this argument.<br>It is too starved a subject for my sword.<br>But [[characters/pandarus\|Pandarus]]—O gods, how do you plague me!<br>I cannot come to Cressid but by Pandar,<br>And he’s as tetchy to be wooed to woo<br>As she is stubborn-chaste against all suit.<br>Tell me, [[characters/apollo\|Apollo]], for thy Daphne’s [[motifs/love\|love]],<br>What Cressid is, what Pandar, and what we?<br>Her bed is India; there she lies, a pearl.<br>Between our Ilium and where she resides<br>Let it be called the wild and wand’ring flood,<br>Ourself the merchant, and this sailing Pandar<br>Our doubtful [[concepts/hope\|hope]], our convoy, and our barque. |
| *(didascalia)* | Alarum. Enter Aeneas |
| AENEAS | How now, Prince [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]]? Wherefore not afield? |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Because not there. This woman’s answer sorts,<br>For womanish it is to be from thence.<br>What news, Aeneas, from the field today? |
| AENEAS | That [[settings/paris\|Paris]] is returned [[motifs/home\|home]], and hurt. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | By whom, Aeneas? |
| AENEAS | [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]], by Menelaus. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Let [[settings/paris\|Paris]] bleed, ’tis but a scar to scorn:<br>[[settings/paris\|Paris]] is gored with Menelaus’ horn. |
| *(didascalia)* | Alarum |
| AENEAS | Hark what good sport is out of town today. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Better at [[motifs/home\|home]], if ‘would I might’ were ‘may’.<br>But to the sport abroad—are you bound thither? |
| AENEAS | In all swift haste. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Come, go we then together. Exeunt |

### Atto 1, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter above [[characters/cressida\|Cressida]] and her servant Alexander |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Who were those went by? |
| ALEXANDER | [[characters/queen\|Queen]] Hecuba and [[characters/helen\|Helen]]. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | And whither go they? |
| ALEXANDER | Up to the eastern tower,<br>Whose height commands as subject all the vale,<br>To see the battle. [[characters/hector\|Hector]], whose patience<br>Is as a virtue fixed, today was moved.<br>He chid Andromache and struck his armourer<br>And, like as there were husbandry in war,<br>Before the sun [[motifs/rose\|rose]] he was harnessed light,<br>And to the field goes he, where every flower<br>Did as a [[archetypes/prophet\|prophet]] weep what it foresaw<br>In [[characters/hector\|Hector]]’s wrath. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | What was his cause of anger? |
| ALEXANDER | The noise goes this: there is among the Greeks<br>A lord of Trojan [[motifs/blood\|blood]], nephew to [[characters/hector\|Hector]];<br>They call him [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]]. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Good, and what of him? |
| ALEXANDER | They say he is a very man per se,<br><br>And stands alone. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | So do all men<br>Unless they are drunk, sick, or have no legs. |
| ALEXANDER | This man, lady, hath robbed many beasts of their particular additions: he is as valiant as the [[motifs/lion\|lion]], churlish as the bear, slow as the elephant—a man into whom [[concepts/nature\|nature]] hath so crowded humours that his valour is crushed into folly, his folly farced with discretion. There is no man hath a virtue that he hath not a glimpse of, nor any man an attaint but he carries some stain of it. He is [[concepts/melancholy\|melancholy]] without cause and merry against the hair; he hath the joints of everything, but everything so out of joint that he is a gouty Briareus, many hands and no use, or purblind Argus, all eyes and no sight. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | But how should this man that makes me smile make [[characters/hector\|Hector]] angry? |
| ALEXANDER | They say he yesterday coped [[characters/hector\|Hector]] in the battle and struck him down, the disdain and shame whereof hath ever since kept [[characters/hector\|Hector]] fasting and waking. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Who comes here? |
| ALEXANDER | Madam, your uncle [[characters/pandarus\|Pandarus]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/pandarus\|Pandarus]] above |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | [[characters/hector\|Hector]]’s a gallant man. |
| ALEXANDER | As may be in the world, lady. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | What’s that? What’s that? |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Good morrow, uncle [[characters/pandarus\|Pandarus]]. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Good morrow, cousin Cressid. What do you talk of?—Good morrow, Alexander.—How do you, cousin? When were you at Ilium? |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | This morning, uncle. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | What were you talking of when I came? Was [[characters/hector\|Hector]] armed and gone ere ye came to Ilium? [[characters/helen\|Helen]] was not up, was she? |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | [[characters/hector\|Hector]] was gone but [[characters/helen\|Helen]] was not up? |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | E’en so. [[characters/hector\|Hector]] was stirring early. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | That were we talking of, and of his anger. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Was he angry? |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | So he says here. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | True, he was so. I know the cause too. He’ll lay about him today, I can tell them that. And there’s [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] will not come far behind him. Let them take heed of [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]], I can tell them that too. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | What, is he angry too? |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Who, [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]]? [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] is the better man of the two. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | O Jupiter! There’s no comparison. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | What, not between [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] and [[characters/hector\|Hector]]? Do you know a man if you see him? |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Ay, if I ever saw him before and knew him. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], I say [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] is [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]]. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Then you say as I say, for I am sure<br>He is not [[characters/hector\|Hector]]. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | No, nor [[characters/hector\|Hector]] is not [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]], in some degrees. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | ’Tis just to each of them: he is himself. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Himself? Alas, poor [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]], I would he were. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | So he is. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Condition I had gone barefoot to India. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | He is not [[characters/hector\|Hector]]. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Himself ? No, he’s not himself. Would a were himself! [[motifs/well\|Well]], the gods are above, [[motifs/time\|time]] must friend or end. [[motifs/well\|Well]], [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]], [[motifs/well\|well]], I would my heart were in her [[concepts/body\|body]]. No, [[characters/hector\|Hector]] is not a better man than [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]]. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Excuse me. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | He is elder. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Pardon me, pardon me. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Th‘other’s not come to’t. You shall tell me another tale when th’other’s come to’t. [[characters/hector\|Hector]] shall not have his will this year. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | He shall not need it if he have his own. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Nor his qualities. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | No matter. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Nor his [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]]. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | ’Twould not become him; his own’s better. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | You have no judgement, niece. [[characters/helen\|Helen]] herself swore th‘other day that [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] for a brown favour, for so ’tis, I must confess—not brown neither— |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | No, but brown. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | [[concepts/faith\|Faith]], to say [[concepts/truth\|truth]], brown and not brown. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | To say the [[concepts/truth\|truth]], true and not true. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | She praised his complexion above [[settings/paris\|Paris]]’. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Why, [[settings/paris\|Paris]] hath colour enough. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | So he has. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Then [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] should have too much. If she praised him above, his complexion is higher than his; he having colour enough, and the other higher, is too flaming a praise for a good complexion. I had as lief [[characters/helen\|Helen]]’s golden tongue had commended [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] for a copper nose. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | I swear to you, I think [[characters/helen\|Helen]] loves him better than [[settings/paris\|Paris]]. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Then she’s a merry Greek indeed. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Nay, I am sure she does. She came to him th’other day into the compassed [[motifs/window\|window]], and you know he has not past three or four hairs on his chin— |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Indeed, a tapster’s arithmetic may soon bring his particulars therein to a total. no |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Why, he is very young—and yet will he within three pound lift as much as his brother [[characters/hector\|Hector]]. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Is he so young a man and so old a lifter? |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | But to prove to you that [[characters/helen\|Helen]] loves him: she came and puts me her white hand to his cloven chin. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Juno have [[concepts/mercy\|mercy]]! How came it cloven? |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Why, you know, ’tis dimpled. I think his smiling becomes him better than any man in all Phrygia. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | O he smiles valiantly. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Does he not? |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | O yes, an’t were a cloud in [[motifs/autumn\|autumn]]. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Why, go to then. But to prove to you that [[characters/helen\|Helen]] loves [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]]— |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] will stand to the proof if you’ll prove it so. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]]? Why, he esteems her no more than I esteem an addle egg. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | If you [[motifs/love\|love]] an addle egg as [[motifs/well\|well]] as you [[motifs/love\|love]] an idle head you would eat chickens i’th’ shell. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | I cannot choose but laugh to think how she tickled his chin. Indeed, she has a marvellous white hand, I must needs confess— |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Without the rack. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | And she takes upon her to spy a white hair on his chin. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Alas, poor chin! Many a wart is richer. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | But there was such laughing! [[characters/queen\|Queen]] Hecuba laughed that her eyes ran o’er. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | With millstones. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | And Cassandra laughed. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | But there was a more temperate [[motifs/fire\|fire]] under the pot of her eyes—or did her eyes run o’er too? |
| PINDARUS | And [[characters/hector\|Hector]] laughed. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | At what was all this laughing? |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Marry, at the white hair that [[characters/helen\|Helen]] spied on<br>[[characters/troilus\|Troilus]]’ chin. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | An’t had been a green hair I should have laughed too. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | They laughed not so much at the hair as at his pretty answer. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | What was his answer? |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Quoth she, ‘Here’s but two-and-fifty hairs on your chin, and one of them is white.’ |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | This is her question. |
| PINDARUS | That’s true, make no question of that. ‘Two-and-fifty hairs,’ quoth he, ‘and one white? That white hair is my father, and all the rest are his sons.’ ‘Jupiter!’ quoth she, ‘which of these hairs is [[settings/paris\|Paris]] my husband?’ ‘The forked one,’ quoth he, ‘pluck’t out and give it him.’ But there was such laughing, and [[characters/helen\|Helen]] so blushed and [[settings/paris\|Paris]] so chafed and all the rest so laughed, that it passed. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | So let it now, for it has been a great while going by. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], cousin, I told you a thing yesterday.<br>Think on’t. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | So I do. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | I’ll be sworn ’tis true. He will weep you an’t were a man born in April. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | And I’ll [[motifs/spring\|spring]] up in his [[motifs/tears\|tears]] an’t were a nettle against May.<br>A retreat is sounded |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Hark, they are coming from the field. Shall we stand up here and see them as they pass toward Ilium? Good niece, do, sweet niece [[characters/cressida\|Cressida]]. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | At your pleasure. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Here, here, here’s an excellent place, here we may see most bravely. I’ll tell you them all by their names as they pass by, but mark [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] above the rest. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Aeneas passing by below |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Speak not so loud. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | That’s Aeneas. Is not that a brave man? He’s one of the [[motifs/flowers\|flowers]] of Troy, I can tell you. But mark [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]]; you shall see anon. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Antenor passing by below |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Who’s that? |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | That’s Antenor. He has a shrewd wit, I can tell you, and he’s a man good enough. He’s one o’th’ soundest judgements in Troy whosoever, and a proper man of person. When comes [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]]? I’ll show you [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] anon. If he see me you shall see him nod at me. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Will he give you the nod? |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | You shall see. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | If he do, the rich shall have more. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/hector\|Hector]] passing by below |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | That’s [[characters/hector\|Hector]], that, that, look you, that. There’s a fellow!—Go thy way, [[characters/hector\|Hector]]!—There’s a brave man, niece. O brave [[characters/hector\|Hector]]! Look how he looks. There’s a countenance. Is’t not a brave man? |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | O a brave man. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Is a not? It does a man’s heart good. Look you what hacks are on his helmet. Look you yonder, do you see? Look you there. There’s no jesting. There’s laying on, take’t off who will, as they say. There be hacks. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Be those with swords? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[settings/paris\|Paris]] passing by below |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Swords, anything, he cares not. An the devil come to him it’s all one. By’God’s lid it does one’s heart good. Yonder comes [[settings/paris\|Paris]], yonder comes [[settings/paris\|Paris]]. Look ye yonder, niece. Is’t not a gallant man too? Is’t not? Why, this is brave now. Who said he came hurt [[motifs/home\|home]] today? He’s not hurt. Why, this will do [[characters/helen\|Helen]]’s heart good now, hal Would I could see [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] now. You shall see [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] anon. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Helenus passing by below |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Who’s that? |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | That’s Helenus. I marvel where [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] is. That’s Helenus. I think he went not forth today. That’s Helenus. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Can Helenus fight, uncle? |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Helenus? No—yes, he’ll fight indifferent [[motifs/well\|well]].<br>I marvel where [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] is. |
| *(didascalia)* | A Shout |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Hark, do you not hear the people cry ‘[[characters/troilus\|Troilus]]’? Helenus is a priest. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] passing by below |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | What sneaking fellow comes yonder? |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Where? Yonder? That’s Deiphobus.—’Tis [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]]! There’s a man, niece, h’m? Brave [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]], the prince of chivalry! |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Peace, for shame, peace. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Mark him, note him. O brave [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]]! Look [[motifs/well\|well]] upon him, niece. Look you how his sword is bloodied and his helm more hacked than [[characters/hector\|Hector]]‘s, and how he looks and how he goes. O admirable youth! He ne’er saw three-and-twenty.—Go thy way, [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]], go thy way!—Had I a sister were a grace, or a daughter a goddess, he should take his choice. O admirable man! [[settings/paris\|Paris]]? [[settings/paris\|Paris]] is dirt to him, and I warrant [[characters/helen\|Helen]] to change would give an eye to boot. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter common soldiers passing by below |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Here comes more. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Asses, fools, dolts. Chaff and bran, chaff and bran. Porridge after meat. I could live and die i‘th’ eyes of [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]]. Ne’er look, ne’er look, the eagles are gone. Crows and daws, crows and daws. I had rather be such a man as [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] than [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]] and all Greece. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | There is among the Greeks [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]], a better man than [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]]. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]]? A drayman, a porter, a very camel. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], [[motifs/well\|well]]. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], [[motifs/well\|well]]? Why, have you any discretion? Have you any eyes? Do you know what a man is? Is not birth, [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]], good shape, discourse, manhood, learning, gentleness, virtue, youth, liberality, and so forth, the spice and salt that season a man? |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Ay, a minced man—and then to be baked with no date in the pie, for then the man’s date is out. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | You are such another woman! One knows not at what ward you lie. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Upon my back to defend my belly, upon my wit to defend my wiles, upon my secrecy to defend mine honesty, my mask to defend my [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]], and you to defend all these—and at all these wards I lie at a thousand watches. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Say one of your watches. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | ‘Nay, I’ll watch you for that’—and that’s one of the chiefest of them too. If I cannot ward what I would not have hit, I can watch you for telling how I took the blow—unless it swell past hiding, and then it’s past watching. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | You are such another! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Boy |
| BOY | Sir, my lord would instantly speak with you. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Where? |
| BOY | At your own house. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Good boy, tell him I come. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Boy |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | I [[concepts/doubt\|doubt]] he be hurt. Fare ye [[motifs/well\|well]], good niece. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Adieu, uncle. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | I’ll be with you, niece, by and by. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | To bring, uncle? |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Ay, a token from [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]]. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | By the same token, you are a bawd. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[characters/pandarus\|Pandarus]] and Alexander |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Words, vows, gifts, [[motifs/tears\|tears]], and [[motifs/love\|love]]’s full sacrifice<br>He offers in another’s enterprise;<br>But more in [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] thousandfold I see<br>Than in the glass of Pandar’s praise may be.<br>Yet hold I off. Women are angels, wooing;<br>Things won are done. Joy’s [[motifs/soul\|soul]] lies in the doing.<br>That she beloved knows naught that knows not this:<br>Men price the thing ungained more than it is.<br>That she was never yet that ever knew<br>[[motifs/love\|Love]] got so sweet as when [[motifs/desire\|desire]] did sue.<br>Therefore this maxim out of [[motifs/love\|love]] I teach:<br>Achievement is command; ungained, beseech.<br>Then though my heart’s contents firm [[motifs/love\|love]] doth bear,<br>Nothing of that shall from mine eyes appear. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |

### Atto 1, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Sennet. Enter [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]], Nestor, [[characters/ulysses\|Ulysses]], Diomedes, and Menelaus, with others |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | Princes, what grief hath set the jaundice on your<br>cheeks?<br>The ample proposition that [[concepts/hope\|hope]] makes<br>In all designs begun on earth below<br>Fails in the promised largeness. Checks and disasters<br>Grow in the veins of actions highest reared,<br>As knots, by the conflux of meeting sap,<br>Infects the sound pine and diverts his grain<br>Tortive and errant from his course of growth.<br>Nor, princes, is it matter new to us<br>That we come short of our suppose so far<br>That after seven years’ siege yet Troy walls stand,<br>Sith every action that hath gone before,<br>Whereof we have record, trial did draw<br>Bias and thwart, not answering the aim<br>And that unbodied figure of the thought<br>That gave’t surmised shape. Why then, you princes,<br>Do you with cheeks abashed behold our works,<br>And think them shames, which are indeed naught else<br>But the protractive trials of great Jove<br>To find persistive constancy in men?<br>The fineness of which mettle is not found<br>In fortune’s [[motifs/love\|love]]—for then the bold and coward,<br>The wise and [[archetypes/fool\|fool]], the [[archetypes/artist\|artist]] and unread,<br>The hard and soft, seem all affined and kin.<br>But in the [[motifs/wind\|wind]] and tempest of her frown<br>Distinction with a loud and powerful fan,<br>Puffing at all, winnows the light away,<br>And what hath mass or matter by itself<br>Lies rich in virtue and unminglèd. |
| NESTOR | With due observance of thy godly seat,<br>Great [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]], Nestor shall apply<br>Thy latest words. In the reproof of chance<br>Lies the true proof of men. The sea being smooth,<br>How many shallow bauble-boats dare sail<br>Upon her patient breast, making their way<br>With those of nobler bulk!<br>But let the ruffian Boreas once enrage<br>The gentle Thetis, and anon behold<br>The strong-ribbed barque through liquid [[settings/mountains\|mountains]]<br>cut,<br>Bounding between the two moist elements<br>Like Perseus’ [[motifs/horse\|horse]]. Where’s then the saucy boat<br>Whose weak untimbered sides but even now<br>Co-rivalled [[concepts/greatness\|greatness]]? Either to harbour fled,<br>Or made a toast for Neptune. Even so<br>Doth valour’s show and valour’s worth divide<br>In storms of fortune. For in her ray and brightness<br>The herd hath more annoyance by the breese<br>Than by the tiger; but when the splitting [[motifs/wind\|wind]]<br>Makes flexible the knees of knotted oaks<br>And flies flee under shade, why then the thing of<br>[[concepts/courage\|courage]],<br>As roused with rage, with rage doth sympathize,<br>And with an accent tuned in selfsame key<br>Retorts to chiding fortune. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]],<br>Thou great commander, nerve and bone of Greece,<br>Heart of our numbers, [[motifs/soul\|soul]] and only spirit<br>In whom the tempers and the minds of all<br>Should be shut up, hear what [[characters/ulysses\|Ulysses]] speaks.<br>Besides th’applause and approbation<br>The which, (to [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]]) most mighty for thy place<br>and sway,<br>And thou, (to Nestor) most reverend for thy stretched-out<br>life,<br>I give to both your speeches—which were such<br>As, [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]], every hand of Greece<br>Should hold up high in brass, and such again<br>As, venerable Nestor, hatched in silver,<br>Should with a bond of air, strong as the axle-tree<br>On which the heavens ride, knit all Greeks’ ears<br>To his experienced tongue—yet let it please both,<br>Thou (to [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]]) great, and (to Nestor) wise, to<br>hear [[characters/ulysses\|Ulysses]] speak. |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | Speak, Prince of Ithaca, and be’t of less expect<br>That matter needless, of importless burden,<br>Divide thy lips, than we are confident<br>When rank [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]] opes his mastic jaws<br>We shall hear music, wit, and oracle. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | Troy, yet upon his basis, had been down<br>And the great [[characters/hector\|Hector]]’s sword had lacked a master<br>But for these instances:<br>The specialty of rule hath been neglected.<br>And look how many Grecian tents do stand<br>Hollow upon this plain: so many hollow factions.<br>When that the general is not like the hive<br>To whom the foragers shall all repair,<br>What honey is expected? Degree being vizarded,<br>Th’unworthiest shows as fairly in the [[forms/masque\|masque]] |
| *(didascalia)* |  . |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | The heavens themselves, the planets, and this centre<br>Observe degree, priority, and place,<br>Infixture, course, proportion, season, form,<br>Office and custom, in all line of order.<br>And therefore is the glorious planet Sol<br>In noble eminence enthroned and sphered<br>Amidst the other, whose med‘cinable eye<br>Corrects the ill aspects of planets evil<br>And posts like the commandment of a [[archetypes/king\|king]],<br>Sans check, to good and bad. But when the planets<br>In evil mixture to disorder wander,<br>What plagues and what portents, what mutiny?<br>What raging of the sea, shaking of earth?<br>Commotion in the winds, frights, changes, horrors<br>Divert and crack, rend and deracinate<br>The unity and married calm of states<br>Quite from their fixture. O when degree is shaked,<br>Which is the ladder to all high designs,<br>The enterprise is sick. How could communities,<br>Degrees in schools, and brotherhoods in cities,<br>Peaceful commerce from dividable shores,<br>The primogenity and due of birth,<br>Prerogative of age, crowns, sceptres, laurels,<br>But by degree stand in authentic place?<br>Take but degree away, untune that string,<br>And hark what discord follows. Each thing meets no<br>In mere oppugnancy. The bounded waters<br>Should lift their bosoms higher than the shores<br>And make a sop of all this solid globe;<br>Strength should be lord of imbecility,<br>And the rude son should strike his father dead.<br>Force should be right—or rather, right and wrong,<br>Between whose endless jar justice resides,<br>Should lose their names, and so should justice too.<br>Then everything includes itself in power,<br>Power into will, will into appetite;<br>And appetite, an universal wolf,<br>So doubly seconded with will and power,<br>Must make perforce an universal prey,<br>And last eat up himself. Great [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]],<br>This chaos, when degree is suffocate,<br>Follows the choking.<br>And this neglection of degree it is<br>That by a pace goes backward in a purpose<br>It hath to climb. The general’s disdained<br>By him one step below; he, by the next;<br>That next, by him beneath. So every step,<br>Exampled by the first pace that is sick<br>Of his superior, grows to an envious fever<br>Of pale and bloodless emulation.<br>And ’tis this fever that keeps Troy on foot,<br>Not her own sinews. To end a tale of length:<br>Troy in our weakness lives, not in her strength. |
| NESTOR | Most wisely hath [[characters/ulysses\|Ulysses]] here discovered<br>The fever whereof all our power is sick. |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | The [[concepts/nature\|nature]] of the sickness found, [[characters/ulysses\|Ulysses]],<br>What is the remedy? |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | The great [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]], whom opinion crowns<br>The sinew and the forehand of our host,<br>Having his ear full of his airy fame<br>Grows dainty of his worth, and in his tent<br>Lies mocking our designs. With him Patroclus<br>Upon a lazy bed the livelong day<br>Breaks scurrile jests<br>And, with ridiculous and awkward action<br>Which, slanderer, he ‘imitation’ calls,<br>He pageants us. Sometime, great [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]],<br>Thy topless deputation he puts on,<br>And like a strutting player, whose conceit<br>Lies in his hamstring and doth think it rich<br>To hear the wooden dialogue and sound<br>’Twixt his stretched footing and the scaffoldage,<br>Such to-be-pitied and o‘er-wrested seeming<br>He acts thy [[concepts/greatness\|greatness]] in. And when he speaks<br>’Tis like a chime a-mending, with terms unsquared<br>Which from the tongue of roaring Typhon dropped<br>Would seem hyperboles. At this fusty stuff<br>The large [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]] on his pressed bed lolling<br>From his deep chest laughs out a loud applause,<br>Cries ‘Excellent! ’Tis [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]] just.<br>Now play me Nestor, hem and stroke thy beard,<br>As he being dressed to some oration.’<br>That’s done as near as the extremest ends<br>Of parallels, as like as Vulcan and his wife.<br>Yet god [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]] still cries, ‘Excellent!<br>‘Tis Nestor right. Now play him me, Patroclus,<br>Arming to answer in a [[motifs/night\|night]] alarm’.<br>And then forsooth the faint defects of age<br>Must be the scene of mirth: to cough and spit,<br>And with a palsy, fumbling on his gorget,<br>Shake in and out the rivet. And at this sport<br>Sir Valour dies, cries, ‘O enough, Patroclus!<br>Or give me ribs of steel. I shall split all<br>In pleasure of my spleen.’ And in this fashion<br>All our abilities, gifts, natures, shapes,<br>Severals and generals of grace exact,<br>Achievements, plots, orders, preventions,<br>Excitements to the field or speech for truce,<br>Success or loss, what is or is not, serves<br>As stuff for these two to make paradoxes. |
| NESTOR | And in the imitation of these twain<br>Who, as [[characters/ulysses\|Ulysses]] says, opinion crowns<br>With an imperial voice, many are infect.<br>[[characters/ajax\|Ajax]] is grown [[concepts/self\|self]]-willed and bears his head<br>In such a rein, in full as proud a place<br>As broad [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]], and keeps his tent like him,<br>Makes factious feasts, rails on our state of war<br>Bold as an oracle, and sets [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]],<br>A slave whose gall coins slanders like a mint,<br>To match us in comparisons with dirt,<br>To weaken and discredit our exposure,<br>How rank so ever rounded in with danger. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | They tax our policy and call it cowardice,<br>Count wisdom as no member of the war,<br>Forestall prescience and esteem no act<br>But that of hand. The still and mental parts<br>That do contrive how many hands shall strike<br>When fitness calls them on, and know by measure<br>Of their observant toil the enemy’s weight,<br>Why, this hath not a finger’s dignity.<br>They call this ‘bed-work’, ‘mapp’ry’, ‘closet war’.<br>So that the ram that batters down the wall,<br>For the great swinge and rudeness of his poise<br>They place before his hand that made the engine,<br>Or those that with the finesse of their souls<br>By reason guide his execution. |
| NESTOR | Let this be granted, and [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]]’ [[motifs/horse\|horse]]<br>Makes many Thetis’ sons. |
| *(didascalia)* | Tucket |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | What trumpet?<br>Look, Menelaus. |
| MENELAUS | From Troy. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Aeneas and a trumpeter |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | What would you fore our tent? |
| AENEAS | Is this great [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]]’s tent I pray you? |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | Even this. |
| AENEAS | May one that is a herald and a prince<br>Do a fair message to his kingly ears? |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | With surety stronger than [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]]’ arm,<br>Fore all the Greekish heads, which with one voice<br>Call [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]] heart and general. |
| AENEAS | Fair leave and large security. How may<br>A stranger to those most imperial looks<br>Know them from eyes of other mortals? |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | How? |
| AENEAS | Ay, I ask that I might waken reverence<br>And on the cheek be ready with a blush<br>Modest as morning when she coldly eyes<br>The youthful Phoebus.<br>Which is that god in office, guiding men?<br>Which is the high and mighty [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]]? |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | (to the Greeks)<br>This Trojan scorns us, or the men of Troy<br>Are ceremonious courtiers. |
| AENEAS | Courtiers as free, as debonair, unarmed,<br>As bending angels—that’s their fame in peace.<br>But when they would seem soldiers they have galls,<br>Good arms, strong joints, true swords—and great<br>Jove’s acorn<br>Nothing so full of heart. But peace, Aeneas,<br>Peace, Trojan; lay thy finger on thy lips.<br>The worthiness of praise distains his worth,<br>If that the praised himself bring the praise forth.<br>But what, repining, the enemy commends,<br>That breath fame blows; that praise, sole pure,<br>transcends. |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | Sir, you of Troy, call you yourself Aeneas? |
| AENEAS | Ay, Greek, that is my name. |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | What’s your affair, I pray you? |
| AENEAS | Sir, pardon, ’tis for [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]]’s ears. |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | He hears naught privately that comes from Troy. |
| AENEAS | Nor I from Troy come not to whisper him.<br>I bring a trumpet to awake his ear,<br>To set his sense on the attentive bent,<br>And then to speak. |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | Speak frankly as the [[motifs/wind\|wind]].<br>It is not [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]]’s sleeping hour.<br>That thou shalt know, Trojan, he is awake,<br>He tells thee so himself. |
| AENEAS | Trumpet, blow loud.<br>Send thy brass voice through all these lazy tents,<br>And every Greek of mettle let him know<br>What Troy means fairly shall be spoke aloud.<br>The trumpet sounds<br>We have, great [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]], here in Troy<br>A prince called [[characters/hector\|Hector]]—Priam is his father—<br>Who in this dull and long-continued truce<br>Is resty grown. He bade me take a trumpet<br>And to this purpose speak: ‘Kings, princes, lords,<br>If there be one among the fair’st of Greece<br>That holds his [[concepts/honour\|honour]] higher than his ease,<br>That seeks his praise more than he fears his peril,<br>That knows his valour and knows not his [[concepts/fear\|fear]],<br>That loves his mistress more than in confession<br>With truant vows to her own lips he loves,<br>And dare avow her [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]] and her worth<br>In other arms than hers—to him this challenge.<br>[[characters/hector\|Hector]] in view of Trojans and of Greeks<br>Shall make it good, or do his best to do it:<br>He hath a lady wiser, fairer, truer,<br>Than ever Greek did compass in his arms,<br>And will tomorrow with his trumpet call<br>Midway between your tents and walls of Troy<br>To rouse a Grecian that is true in [[motifs/love\|love]].<br>If any come, [[characters/hector\|Hector]] shall [[concepts/honour\|honour]] him.<br>If none, he’ll say in Troy when he retires<br>The Grecian dames are sunburnt and not worth<br>The splinter of a lance.’ Even so much. |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | This shall be told our lovers, Lord Aeneas.<br>If none of them have [[motifs/soul\|soul]] in such a kind,<br>We left them all at [[motifs/home\|home]]. But we are soldiers,<br>And may that soldier a mere recreant prove<br>That means not, hath not, or is not in [[motifs/love\|love]].<br>If then one is, or hath, or means to be,<br>That one meets [[characters/hector\|Hector]]. If none else, I’ll be he. |
| NESTOR | (to<br>Aeneas)<br>Tell him of Nestor, one that was a man<br>When [[characters/hector\|Hector]]’s grandsire sucked. He is old now,<br>But if there be not in our Grecian mould<br>One noble man that hath one spark of [[motifs/fire\|fire]]<br>To answer for his [[motifs/love\|love]], tell him from me<br>I’ll hide my silver beard in a gold beaver<br>And in my vambrace put this withered brawn,<br>And meeting him will tell him that my lady<br>Was fairer than his grandam, and as chaste<br>As may be in the world. His youth in flood,<br>I’ll prove this [[concepts/truth\|truth]] with my three drops of [[motifs/blood\|blood]]. |
| AENEAS | Now heavens forbid such scarcity of youth. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | Amen. |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | Fair Lord Aeneas, let me touch your hand.<br>To our pavilion shall I lead you first.<br>[[characters/achilles\|Achilles]] shall have word of this intent;<br>So shall each lord of Greece, from tent to tent.<br>Yourself shall feast with us before you go,<br>And find the welcome of a noble foe. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt all but [[characters/ulysses\|Ulysses]] and Nestor |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | Nestor! |
| NESTOR | What says [[characters/ulysses\|Ulysses]]? |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | I have a young<br>Conception in my brain; be you my [[motifs/time\|time]]<br>To bring it to some shape. |
| NESTOR | What is’t? |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | This ’tis:<br>Blunt wedges rive hard knots. The seeded [[concepts/pride\|pride]]<br>That hath to this maturity blown up<br>In rank [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]] must or now be cropped<br>Or, shedding, breed a nursery of like evil<br>To overbulk us all. |
| NESTOR | [[motifs/well\|Well]], and how? |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | This challenge that the gallant [[characters/hector\|Hector]] sends,<br>However it is spread in general name,<br>Relates in purpose only to [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]]. |
| NESTOR | The purpose is perspicuous, even as substance<br>Whose grossness little characters sum up.<br>And, in the publication, make no strain<br>But that [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]], were his brain as barren<br>As banks of Libya—though, [[characters/apollo\|Apollo]] knows,<br>’Tis dry enough—will with great [[characters/speed\|speed]] of judgement,<br>Ay with celerity, find [[characters/hector\|Hector]]’s purpose<br>Pointing on him. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | And wake him to the answer, think you? |
| NESTOR | Yes, ‘tis most meet. Who may you else oppose,<br>That can from [[characters/hector\|Hector]] bring his [[concepts/honour\|honour]] off,<br>If not [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]]? Though’t be a sportful combat,<br>Yet in this trial much opinion dwells,<br>For here the Trojans taste our dear’st repute<br>With their fin‘st palate. And trust to me, [[characters/ulysses\|Ulysses]],<br>Our imputation shall be oddly poised<br>In this wild action: for the success,<br>Although particular, shall give a scantling<br>Of good or bad unto the general—<br>And in such indices, although small pricks<br>To their subsequent volumes, there is seen<br>The baby figure of the giant mass<br>Of things to come at large. It is supposed<br>He that meets [[characters/hector\|Hector]] issues from our choice,<br>And choice, being mutual act of all our souls,<br>Makes merit her election, and doth boil,<br>As ’twere, from forth us all a man distilled<br>Out of our virtues—who miscarrying,<br>What heart from hence receives the conqu‘ring part<br>To steel a strong opinion to themselves?<br>Which entertained, limbs are e’en his instruments,<br>In no less working than are swords and bows<br>Directive by the limbs. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | Give pardon to my speech:<br>Therefore ’tis meet [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]] meet not [[characters/hector\|Hector]].<br>Let us like merchants show our foulest wares<br>And think perchance they’ll sell. If not,<br>The lustre of the better yet to show<br>Shall show the better. Do not consent<br>That ever [[characters/hector\|Hector]] and [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]] meet,<br>For both our [[concepts/honour\|honour]] and our shame in this<br>Are dogged with two strange followers. |
| NESTOR | I see them not with my old eyes. What are they? |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | What glory our [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]] shares from [[characters/hector\|Hector]],<br>Were he not proud we all should wear with him.<br>But he already is too insolent,<br>And we were better parch in Afric sun<br>Than in the [[concepts/pride\|pride]] and salt scorn of his eyes,<br>Should he scape [[characters/hector\|Hector]] fair. If he were foiled,<br>Why then we did our main opinion crush<br>In taint of our best man. No, make a lott’ry,<br>And by device let blockish [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]] draw<br>The sort to fight with [[characters/hector\|Hector]]. Among ourselves<br>Give him allowance as the worthier man—<br>For that will physic the great Myrmidon,<br>Who broils in loud applause, and make him fall<br>His crest, that prouder than blue Iris bends.<br>If the dull brainless [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]] come safe off,<br>We’ll dress him up in voices; if he fail,<br>Yet go we under our opinion still<br>That we have better men. But hit or miss,<br>Our project’s life this shape of sense assumes:<br>[[characters/ajax\|Ajax]] employed plucks down [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]]’ plumes. |
| NESTOR | Now, [[characters/ulysses\|Ulysses]], I begin to relish thy advice,<br>And I will give a taste of it forthwith<br>To [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]]. Go we to him straight.<br>Two curs shall tame each other; [[concepts/pride\|pride]] alone<br>Must tarre the mastiffs on, as ’twere their bone. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 2, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]] and [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]] |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]]. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]]—how if he had boils, full, all over, generally? |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]]. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | And those boils did run? Say so, did not the General run then? Were not that a botchy core? |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | Dog. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Then there would come some matter from him.<br>I see none now. |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | Thou bitch-wolf’s son, canst thou not hear? Feel then.<br>He strikes [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]] |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | The plague of Greece upon thee, thou mongrel beef-witted lord! |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | Speak then, thou unsifted leaven, speak! I will beat thee into handsomeness. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | I shall sooner rail thee into wit and holiness. But I think thy [[motifs/horse\|horse]] will sooner con an oration than thou learn a [[motifs/prayer\|prayer]] without book. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]] strikes him |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Thou canst strike, canst thou? A red murrain o’ thy jade’s tricks. |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | Toad’s stool! |
| *(didascalia)* | He strikes [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]] |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | Learn me the proclamation. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Dost thou think I have no sense, thou strikest me thus? |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | The proclamation. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Thou art proclaimed a [[archetypes/fool\|fool]], I think. |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | Do not, porcupine, do not. My fingers itch. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | I would thou didst itch from head to foot. An I had the scratching of thee, I would make thee the loathsomest scab in Greece. |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | I say, the proclamation. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Thou grumblest and railest every hour on [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]], and thou art as full of envy at his [[concepts/greatness\|greatness]] as Cerberus is at Proserpina’s [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]], ay, that thou barkest at him. |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | Mistress [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]]. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Thou shouldst strike him. |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | Cobloaf. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | He would pun thee into shivers with his fist, as a sailor breaks a biscuit. |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | You whoreson cur. |
| *(didascalia)* | He strikes [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]] |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Do! Do! |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | Thou stool for a [[characters/witch\|witch]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | He strikes [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]] |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Ay, do, do! Thou sodden-witted lord, thou hast in thy skull no more brain than I have in mine elbows. An asnico may tutor thee. Thou scurvy valiant ass, thou art here but to thrash Trojans, and thou art bought and sold among those of any wit like a barbarian slave. If thou use to beat me, I will begin at thy heel and tell what thou art by inches, thou thing of no bowels, thou. |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | You dog. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | You scurvy lord. |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | You cur. |
| *(didascalia)* | He strikes [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]] |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Mars his idiot! Do, rudeness! Do, camel, do, do! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]] and Patroclus |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | Why, how now, [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]]? Wherefore do ye thus?<br>How now, [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]]? What’s the matter, man? |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | You see him there? Do you? |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | Ay. What’s the matter? |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Nay, look upon him. |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | So I do. What’s the matter? |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Nay, but regard him [[motifs/well\|well]]. |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | ‘[[motifs/well\|Well]]’? Why, I do so. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | But yet you look not [[motifs/well\|well]] upon him. For whosomever you take him to be, he is [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]]. |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | I know that, [[archetypes/fool\|fool]]. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Ay, but ‘that [[archetypes/fool\|fool]]’ knows not himself. |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | Therefore I beat thee. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Lo, lo, lo, lo, what modicums of wit he utters.<br>His evasions have ears thus long. I have bobbed his brain more than he has beat my bones. I will buy nine sparrows for a penny, and his pia mater is not worth the ninth part of a sparrow. This lord, [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]]—[[characters/ajax\|Ajax]], who wears his wit in his belly and his guts in his head—I’ll tell you what I say of him. |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | What? |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | I say, this [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]]— |
| *(didascalia)* | [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]] threatens to strike him |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | Nay, good [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]]. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Has not so much wit— |
| *(didascalia)* | [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]] threatens to strike him |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | (to [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]]) Nay, I must hold you. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | As will stop the eye of [[characters/helen\|Helen]]’s needle, for whom he comes to fight. |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | Peace, [[archetypes/fool\|fool]]. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | I would have peace and quietness, but the [[archetypes/fool\|fool]] will not. He, there, that he, look you there. |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | O thou damned cur I shall— |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | (to [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]]) Will you set your wit to a [[archetypes/fool\|fool]]’s? |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | No, I warrant you, for a [[archetypes/fool\|fool]]’s will shame it. |
| PATROCLUS | Good words, [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]]. |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | (to [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]]) What’s the quarrel? |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | I bade the vile owl go learn me the tenor of the proclamation, and he rails upon me. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | I serve thee not. |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], go to, go to. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | I serve here voluntary. |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | Your last service was sufferance. ‘Twas not voluntary: no man is beaten voluntary. [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]] was here the voluntary, and you as under an impress. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | E’en so. A great deal of your wit, too, lies in your sinews, or else there be liars. [[characters/hector\|Hector]] shall have a great catch an a knock out either of your brains. A were as good crack a fusty nut with no kernel. |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | What, with me too, [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]]? |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | There’s [[characters/ulysses\|Ulysses]] and old Nestor, whose wit was mouldy ere your grandsires had nails on their toes, yoke you like draught oxen and make you plough up the war. |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | What? What? |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Yes, good sooth. To [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]]! To, [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]], to— |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | I shall cut out your tongue. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | ’Tis no matter. I shall speak as much wit as thou afterwards. |
| PATROCLUS | No more words, [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]], peace. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | I will hold my peace when [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]]’ brach bids me, shall I? |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | There’s for you, Patroclus. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | I will see you hanged like clodpolls ere I come any more to your tents. I will keep where there is wit stirring, and leave the faction of fools. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| PATROCLUS | A good riddance. |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | (to [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]])<br>Marry, this, sir, is proclaimed through all our host:<br>That [[characters/hector\|Hector]], by the fifth hour of the sun,<br>Will with a trumpet ‘twixt our tents and Troy<br>Tomorrow morning call some knight to arms<br>That hath a stomach, and such a one that dare<br>Maintain—I know not what. ’Tis trash. Farewell. |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | Farewell. Who shall answer him? |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | I know not. ‘Tis put to lott’ry. Otherwise, |
| *(didascalia)* | He knew his man. Exeunt [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]] and Patroclus |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | O, meaning you? I will go learn more of it. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| *(didascalia)* | 2.2 Sennet. Enter [[archetypes/king\|King]] Priam, [[characters/hector\|Hector]], [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]], [[settings/paris\|Paris]], and Helenus |
| PRIAM | After so many hours, lives, speeches spent,<br>Thus once again says Nestor from the Greeks:<br>‘Deliver [[characters/helen\|Helen]], and all damage else—<br>As [[concepts/honour\|honour]], loss of [[motifs/time\|time]], travail, expense,<br>Wounds, friends, and what else dear that is consumed<br>In hot digestion of this cormorant war—<br>Shall be struck off.’ [[characters/hector\|Hector]], what say you to’t? |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Though no man lesser fears the Greeks than I,<br>As far as toucheth my particular, yet, dread Priam,<br>There is no lady of more softer bowels,<br>More spongy to suck in the sense of [[concepts/fear\|fear]],<br>More ready to cry out, ‘Who knows what follows?’<br>Than [[characters/hector\|Hector]] is. The wound of peace is surety,<br>Surety secure; but modest [[concepts/doubt\|doubt]] is called<br>The beacon of the wise, the tent that searches<br>To th’ [[characters/bottom\|bottom]] of the worst. Let [[characters/helen\|Helen]] go.<br>Since the first sword was drawn about this question,<br>Every tithe-[[motifs/soul\|soul]], ’mongst many thousand dimes,<br>Hath been as dear as [[characters/helen\|Helen]]—I mean, of ours.<br>If we have lost so many tenths of ours<br>To guard a thing not ours—nor worth to us,<br>Had it our name, the value of one ten—<br>What merit’s in that reason which denies<br>The yielding of her up? |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Fie, fie, my brother!<br>Weigh you the worth and [[concepts/honour\|honour]] of a [[archetypes/king\|king]]<br>So great as our dread father in a scale<br>Of common ounces? Will you with counters sum<br>The past-proportion of his infinite,<br>And buckle in a waist most fathomless<br>With spans and inches so diminutive<br>As fears and reasons? Fie, for godly shame! |
| HELENUS | No marvel though you bite so sharp at reasons,<br>You are so empty of them. Should not our father<br>Bear the great sway of his affairs with reason<br>Because your speech hath none that tells him so? |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | You are for [[motifs/dreams\|dreams]] and slumbers, brother priest.<br>You fur your gloves with ‘reason’. Here are your<br>reasons:<br>You know an enemy intends you harm,<br>You know a sword employed is perilous,<br>And reason flies the object of all harm.<br>Who marvels then, when Helenus beholds<br>A Grecian and his sword, if he do set<br>The very wings of reason to his heels<br>And fly like chidden Mercury from Jove,<br>Or like a star disorbed? Nay, if we talk of reason,<br>Let’s shut our gates and [[motifs/sleep\|sleep]]. Manhood and [[concepts/honour\|honour]]<br>Should have hare hearts, would they but fat their<br>thoughts<br>With this crammed reason. Reason and respect<br>Make livers pale and lustihood deject. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Brother, she is not worth what she doth cost<br>The holding. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | What’s aught but as ’tis valued? |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | But value dwells not in particular will.<br>It holds his estimate and dignity<br>As [[motifs/well\|well]] wherein ‘tis precious of itself<br>As in the prizer. ’Tis mad idolatry<br>To make the service greater than the god;<br>And the will dotes that is inclinable<br>To what infectiously itself affects<br>Without some image of th’affected merit. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | I take today a wife, and my election<br>Is led on in the conduct of my will;<br>My will enkindled by mine eyes and ears,<br>Two traded pilots ‘twixt the dangerous shores<br>Of will and judgement. How may I avoid—<br>Although my will distaste what it elected—<br>The wife I chose? There can be no evasion<br>To blench from this and to stand firm by [[concepts/honour\|honour]].<br>We turn not back the silks upon the merchant<br>When we have spoiled them; nor the remainder viands<br>We do not throw in unrespective sewer<br>Because we now are full. It was thought meet<br>[[settings/paris\|Paris]] should do some vengeance on the Greeks.<br>Your breath of full consent bellied his sails;<br>The seas and winds, old wranglers, took a truce<br>And did him service. He touched the ports desired,<br>And for an old aunt whom the Greeks held captive<br>He brought a Grecian [[characters/queen\|queen]], whose youth and freshness<br>Wrinkles [[characters/apollo\|Apollo]]’s and makes stale the morning.<br>Why keep we her? The Grecians keep our aunt.<br>Is she worth keeping? Why, she is a pearl<br>Whose price hath launched above a thousand ships<br>And turned crowned kings to merchants.<br>If you’ll avouch ’twas wisdom [[settings/paris\|Paris]] went—<br>As you must needs, for you all cried, ‘Go, go!’;<br>If you’ll confess he brought [[motifs/home\|home]] noble prize—<br>As you must needs, for you all clapped your hands<br>And cried, ‘Inestimable!’—why do you now<br>The issue of your proper wisdoms rate,<br>And do a deed that never fortune did:<br>Beggar the estimation which you prized<br>Richer than sea and land? O theft most base,<br>That we have stol’n what we do [[concepts/fear\|fear]] to keep!<br>But thieves unworthy of a thing so stol’n,<br>That in their country did them that disgrace<br>We [[concepts/fear\|fear]] to warrant in our native place. |
| *(didascalia)* | CASSANDRA within |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Cry, Trojans, cry! |
| PRIAM | What noise? What shriek is this? |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | ’Tis our mad sister. I do know her voice. |
| *(didascalia)* | CASSANDRA within Cry, Trojans! |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | It is Cassandra. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Cassandra raving, with her hair about her ears |
| CASSANDRA | Cry, Trojans, cry! Lend me ten thousand eyes<br>And I will fill them with prophetic [[motifs/tears\|tears]]. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Peace, sister, peace. |
| CASSANDRA | Virgins and boys, mid-age, and wrinkled old,<br>Soft infancy that nothing canst but cry,<br>Add to my clamours. Let us pay betimes<br>A moiety of that mass of moan to come.<br>Cry, Trojans, cry! Practise your eyes with [[motifs/tears\|tears]].<br>Troy must not be, nor goodly Ilium stand.<br>Our firebrand brother, [[settings/paris\|Paris]], burns us all.<br>Cry, Trojans, cry! Ah [[characters/helen\|Helen]], and ah woe!<br>Cry, cry ‘Troy burns!’—or else let [[characters/helen\|Helen]] go. Exit |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Now, youthful [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]], do not these high strains<br>Of divination in our sister work<br>Some touches of remorse? Or is your [[motifs/blood\|blood]]<br>So madly hot that no discourse of reason,<br>Nor [[concepts/fear\|fear]] of bad success in a bad cause,<br>Can qualify the same? |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Why, brother [[characters/hector\|Hector]],<br>We may not think the justness of each act<br>Such and no other than the event doth form it,<br>Nor once deject the [[concepts/courage\|courage]] of our minds<br>Because Cassandra’s mad. Her brainsick raptures<br>Cannot distaste the goodness of a quarrel<br>Which hath our several honours all engaged<br>To make it gracious. For my private part,<br>I am no more touched than all Priam’s sons.<br>And Jove forbid there should be done amongst us<br>Such things as might offend the weakest spleen<br>To fight for and maintain. |
| [[settings/paris\|PARIS]] | Else might the world convince of levity<br>As [[motifs/well\|well]] my undertakings as your counsels.<br>But I attest the gods, your full consent<br>Gave wings to my propension and cut off<br>All fears attending on so dire a project.<br>For what, alas, can these my single arms?<br>What propugnation is in one man’s valour<br>To stand the push and enmity of those<br>This quarrel would excite? Yet I protest,<br>Were I alone to pass the difficulties<br>And had as ample power as I have will,<br>[[settings/paris\|Paris]] should ne’er retract what he hath done<br>Nor faint in the pursuit. |
| PRIAM | [[settings/paris\|Paris]], you speak<br>Like one besotted on your sweet delights.<br>You have the honey still, but these the gall.<br>So to be valiant is no praise at all. |
| [[settings/paris\|PARIS]] | Sir, I propose not merely to myself<br>The pleasures such a [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]] brings with it,<br>But I would have the soil of her fair rape<br>Wiped off in honourable keeping her.<br>What treason were it to the ransacked [[characters/queen\|queen]],<br>Disgrace to your great worths, and shame to me,<br>Now to deliver her possession up<br>On terms of base compulsion? Can it be<br>That so degenerate a strain as this<br>Should once set footing in your generous bosoms?<br>There’s not the meanest spirit on our party<br>Without a heart to dare or sword to draw<br>When [[characters/helen\|Helen]] is defended; nor none so noble<br>Whose life were ill bestowed or [[motifs/death\|death]] unfamed<br>Where [[characters/helen\|Helen]] is the subject. Then I say:<br>[[motifs/well\|Well]] may we fight for her whom we know [[motifs/well\|well]]<br>The world’s large spaces cannot parallel. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | [[settings/paris\|Paris]] and [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]], you have both said [[motifs/well\|well]],<br>But on the cause and question now in hand<br>Have glossed but superficially—not much<br>Unlike young men, whom Aristotle thought<br>Unfit to hear moral philosophy.<br>The reasons you allege do more conduce<br>To the hot passion of distempered [[motifs/blood\|blood]]<br>Than to make up a free determination<br>‘Twixt right and wrong; for pleasure and [[concepts/revenge\|revenge]]<br>Have ears more deaf than adders to the voice<br>Of any true decision. [[concepts/nature\|Nature]] craves<br>All dues be rendered to their owners. Now,<br>What nearer debt in all humanity<br>Than wife is to the husband? If this law<br>Of [[concepts/nature\|nature]] be corrupted through affection,<br>And that great minds, of partial indulgence<br>To their benumbed wills, resist the same,<br>There is a law in each [[motifs/well\|well]]-ordered nation<br>To curb those raging appetites that are<br>Most disobedient and refractory.<br>If [[characters/helen\|Helen]] then be wife to Sparta’s [[archetypes/king\|king]],<br>As it is known she is, these moral laws<br>Of [[concepts/nature\|nature]] and of nations speak aloud<br>To have her back returned. Thus to persist<br>In doing wrong extenuates not wrong,<br>But makes it much more heavy. [[characters/hector\|Hector]]’s opinion<br>Is this in way of [[concepts/truth\|truth]]—yet ne’ertheless,<br>My sprightly brethren, I propend to you<br>In resolution to keep [[characters/helen\|Helen]] still;<br>For ’tis a cause that hath no mean dependence<br>Upon our joint and several dignities. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Why, there you touched the life of our design.<br>Were it not glory that we more affected<br>Than the performance of our heaving spleens,<br>I would not wish a drop of Trojan [[motifs/blood\|blood]]<br>Spent more in her defence. But, worthy [[characters/hector\|Hector]],<br>She is a theme of [[concepts/honour\|honour]] and renown,<br>A spur to valiant and magnanimous deeds,<br>Whose present [[concepts/courage\|courage]] may beat down our foes,<br>And fame in [[motifs/time\|time]] to come canonize us—<br>For I presume brave [[characters/hector\|Hector]] would not lose<br>So rich advantage of a promised glory<br>As smiles upon the forehead of this action<br>For the wide world’s revenue. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | I am yours,<br>You valiant offspring of great Priamus.<br>I have a roisting challenge sent amongst<br>The dull and factious nobles of the Greeks<br>Will shriek amazement to their drowsy spirits.<br>I was advertised their great general slept<br>Whilst emulation in the army crept;<br>This I presume will wake him. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Exeunt |

### Atto 2, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]] |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | How now, [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]]? What, lost in the labyrinth of thy fury? Shall the elephant [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]] carry it thus? He beats me and I rail at him. O worthy satisfaction! Would it were otherwise: that I could beat him whilst he railed at me. ‘Sfoot, I’ll learn to conjure and raise devils but I’ll see some issue of my spiteful execrations. Then there’s [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]]: a rare engineer. If Troy be not taken till these two undermine it, the walls will stand till they fall of themselves. O thou great thunder-darter of Olympus, forget that thou art Jove, the [[archetypes/king\|king]] of gods; and Mercury, lose all the serpentine craft of thy caduceus, if ye take not that little, little, less than little wit from them that they have—which short-armed ignorance itself knows is so abundant-scarce it will not in circumvention deliver a fly from a spider without drawing their massy irons and cutting the web. After this, the vengeance on the whole camp—or rather, the Neapolitan bone-ache, for that methinks is the curse dependent on those that war for a placket. I have said my prayers, and devil Envy say ‘Amen’.—What ho! My lord [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]]! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter |
| *(didascalia)* | Patroclus at the door to the tent |
| *(didascalia)* | PATROCLUS Who’s there? [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]]? Good [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]], come in and rail. Exit |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | If I could ha’ remembered a gilt counterfeit, thou wouldst not have slipped out of my contemplation; but it is no matter. Thyself upon thyself! The common curse of mankind, folly and ignorance, be thine in great revenue! Heaven bless thee from a tutor, and discipline come not near thee! Let thy [[motifs/blood\|blood]] be thy direction till thy [[motifs/death\|death]]! Then if she that lays thee out says thou art a fair corpse, I’ll be sworn and sworn upon’t she never shrouded any but lazars. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Patroclus |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Amen.—Where’s [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]]? |
| PATROCLUS | What, art thou devout? Wast thou in [[motifs/prayer\|prayer]]? |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Ay. The heavens hear me! |
| PATROCLUS | Amen. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]] |
