| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Grecian, thou dost not use me courteously,<br>To shame the zeal of my petition towards thee<br>In praising her. I tell thee, lord of Greece,<br>She is as far high-soaring o’er thy praises<br>As thou unworthy to be called her servant.<br>I charge thee use her [[motifs/well\|well]], even for my charge;<br>For, by the dreadful Pluto, if thou dost not,<br>Though the great bulk [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]] be thy guard<br>I’ll cut thy throat. |
| DIOMEDES | O be not moved, Prince [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]].<br>Let me be privileged by my place and message<br>To be a speaker free. When I am hence<br>I’ll answer to my lust. And know you, lord,<br>I’ll nothing do on charge. To her own worth<br>She shall be prized; but that you say ‘Be’t so’,<br>I’ll speak it in my spirit and [[concepts/honour\|honour]] ‘No!’ |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Come, to the port.—I’ll tell thee, Diomed,<br>This brave shall oft make thee to hide thy head.—<br>Lady, give me your hand, and as we walk<br>To our own selves bend we our needful talk. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]], [[characters/cressida\|Cressida]], and Diomedes |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | A trumpet sounds |
| [[settings/paris\|PARIS]] | Hark, [[characters/hector\|Hector]]’s trumpet. |
| AENEAS | How have we spent this morning?<br>The Prince must think me tardy and remiss,<br>That swore to ride before him in the field. |
| [[settings/paris\|PARIS]] | ‘Tis [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]]’ fault. Come, come to field with him. |
| DEIPHOBUS | Let us make ready straight. |
| AENEAS | Yea, with a bridegroom’s fresh alacrity<br>Let us address to tend on [[characters/hector\|Hector]]’s heels.<br>The glory of our Troy doth this day lie<br>On his fair worth and single chivalry. Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 6

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]] armed, [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]], Patroclus, [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]], Menelaus, [[characters/ulysses\|Ulysses]], Nestor, a trumpeter, and others |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | Here art thou in appointment fresh and fair,<br>Anticipating [[motifs/time\|time]] with starting [[concepts/courage\|courage]].<br>Give with thy trumpet a loud note to Troy,<br>Thou dreadful [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]], that the appallèd air<br>May pierce the head of the great combatant<br>And hale him hither. |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | Thou trumpet, there’s my purse.<br>He gives him money<br>Now crack thy lungs and split thy brazen pipe.<br>Blow, [[archetypes/villain\|villain]], till thy spherèd bias cheek<br>Outswell the colic of puffed Aquilon.<br>Come, stretch thy chest and let thy eyes spout [[motifs/blood\|blood]];<br>Thou blow’st for [[characters/hector\|Hector]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | The trumpet sounds |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | No trumpet answers. |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | ’Tis but early days. |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | Is not yond Diomed with Calchas’ daughter? |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | ’Tis he. I ken the manner of his gait.<br>He rises on the toe: that spirit of his<br>In [[concepts/aspiration\|aspiration]] lifts him from the earth. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Diomedes and [[characters/cressida\|Cressida]] |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | (to Diomedes)<br>Is this the Lady Cressid? |
| DIOMEDES | Even she. |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | Most dearly welcome to the Greeks, sweet lady.<br>He kisses her |
| NESTOR | (to [[characters/cressida\|Cressida]])<br>Our General doth salute you with a kiss. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | Yet is the kindness but particular;<br>’Twere better she were kissed in general. |
| NESTOR | And very courtly counsel. I’ll begin.<br>He kisses her<br>So much for Nestor. |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | I’ll take that [[motifs/winter\|winter]] from your lips, fair lady.<br>He kisses her<br>[[characters/achilles\|Achilles]] bids you welcome. |
| MENELAUS | (to [[characters/cressida\|Cressida]])<br>I had good argument for kissing once. |
| PATROCLUS | But that’s no argument for kissing now; |
| *(didascalia)* | For thus stepping between them popped [[settings/paris\|Paris]] in his |
| PATROCLUS | hardiment,<br>And parted thus you and your argument.<br>He kisses her |
| *(didascalia)* | [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] aside |
| PATROCLUS | O deadly gall, and theme of all our scorns!<br>For which we lose our heads to gild his horns. |
| PATROCLUS | (to [[characters/cressida\|Cressida]])<br>The first was Menelaus’ kiss; this, mine.<br>Patroclus kisses you.<br>He kisses her again |
| MENELAUS | O this is trim. |
| PATROCLUS | (to [[characters/cressida\|Cressida]])<br>[[settings/paris\|Paris]] and I kiss evermore for him. |
| MENELAUS | I’ll have my kiss, sir.—Lady, by your leave. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | In kissing do you render or receive? |
| *(didascalia)* | MENELAUS |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Both take and give. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | I’ll make my match to live,<br>The kiss you take is better than you give.<br>Therefore no kiss. |
| MENELAUS | I’ll give you boot: I’ll give you three for one. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | You are an odd man: give even or give none. |
| MENELAUS | An odd man, lady? Every man is odd. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | No, [[settings/paris\|Paris]] is not—for you know ’tis true<br>That you are odd, and he is even with you. |
| MENELAUS | You fillip me o’th’ head. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | No, I’ll be sworn. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | It were no match, your nail against his horn.<br>May I, sweet lady, beg a kiss of you? |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | You may. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | I do [[motifs/desire\|desire]] it. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Why, beg too. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | Why then, for [[characters/venus\|Venus]]’ sake, give me a kiss,<br>When [[characters/helen\|Helen]] is a maid again, and his— |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | I am your debtor; claim it when ’tis due. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | Never’s my day, and then a kiss of you. |
| DIOMEDES | Lady, a word. I’ll bring you to your father. |
| *(didascalia)* | They talk apart |
| NESTOR | A woman of quick sense. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | Fie, fie upon her!<br>There’s language in her eye, her cheek, her lip;<br>Nay, her foot speaks. Her wanton spirits look out<br>At every joint and motive of her [[concepts/body\|body]].<br>O these encounterers so glib of tongue,<br>That give accosting welcome ere it comes,<br>And wide unclasp the tables of their thoughts<br>To every ticklish reader, set them down<br>For sluttish spoils of opportunity<br>And daughters of the game. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Diomedes and [[characters/cressida\|Cressida]] |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish |
| ALL | The Trojans’ trumpet. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter all off Troy: [[characters/hector\|Hector]] armed, [[settings/paris\|Paris]], Aeneas, Helenus, and attendants, among them [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | Yonder comes the troop. |
| *(didascalia)* | AENEAS coming forward |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | Hail, all you state of Greece! What shall be done<br>To him that victory commands? Or do you purpose<br>A victor shall be known? Will you the knights<br>Shall to the edge of all extremity<br>Pursue each other, or shall they be divided<br>By any voice or order of the field?<br>[[characters/hector\|Hector]] bade ask. |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | Which way would [[characters/hector\|Hector]] have it? |
| AENEAS | He cares not; he’ll obey conditions. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] |
| AENEAS | ’Tis done like [[characters/hector\|Hector]]—but securely done,<br>A little proudly, and great deal disprising<br>The knight opposed. |
| AENEAS | If not [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]], sir,<br>What is your name? |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | If not [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]], nothing. |
| AENEAS | Therefore [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]]. But whate’er, know this:<br>In the extremity of great and little,<br>Valour and [[concepts/pride\|pride]] excel themselves in [[characters/hector\|Hector]],<br>The one almost as infinite as all,<br>The other blank as nothing. Weigh him [[motifs/well\|well]],<br>And that which looks like [[concepts/pride\|pride]] is courtesy.<br>This [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]] is half made of [[characters/hector\|Hector]]’s [[motifs/blood\|blood]],<br>In [[motifs/love\|love]] whereof half [[characters/hector\|Hector]] stays at [[motifs/home\|home]].<br>Half heart, half hand, half [[characters/hector\|Hector]] comes to seek<br>This blended knight, half Trojan and half Greek. |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | A maiden battle, then? O I perceive you. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Diomedes |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | Here is Sir Diomed.—Go, gentle knight,<br>Stand by our [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]]. As you and Lord Aeneas<br>Consent upon the order of their fight,<br>So be it: either to the uttermost<br>Or else a breath. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]], Diomedes, [[characters/hector\|Hector]], and Aeneas |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | The combatants being kin<br>Half stints their strife before their strokes begin. [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] They are opposed already. |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | What Trojan is that same that looks so heavy? |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | The youngest son of Priam, a true knight:<br>They call him [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]].<br>Not yet mature, yet matchless-firm of word,<br>Speaking in deeds and deedless in his tongue;<br>Not soon provoked, nor being provoked soon calmed;<br>His heart and hand both open and both free.<br>For what he has he gives; what thinks, he shows;<br>Yet gives he not till judgement guide his bounty,<br>Nor dignifies an impare thought with breath.<br>Manly as [[characters/hector\|Hector]] but more dangerous,<br>For [[characters/hector\|Hector]] in his blaze of wrath subscribes<br>To tender objects, but he in heat of action<br>Is more vindicative than jealous [[motifs/love\|love]].<br>They call him [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]], and on him erect<br>A second [[concepts/hope\|hope]] as fairly built as [[characters/hector\|Hector]].<br>Thus says Aeneas, one that knows the youth<br>Even to his inches, and with private [[motifs/soul\|soul]]<br>Did in great Ilium thus translate him to me. |
| *(didascalia)* | Alarum |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | They are in action. |
| NESTOR | Now, [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]], hold thine own! |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | [[characters/hector\|Hector]], thou steep’st! Awake thee! |
| *(didascalia)* | His blows are [[motifs/well\|well]] disposed. There, [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]]! Exeunt |
| *(didascalia)* | 4.7 Enter [[characters/hector\|Hector]] and [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]] fighting, and Aeneas and |
| *(didascalia)* | Diomedes interposing. Trumpets cease |
| DIOMEDES | You must no more. |
| AENEAS | Princes, enough, so please you. |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | I am not warm yet. Let us fight again. |
| DIOMEDES | As [[characters/hector\|Hector]] pleases. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Why then will I no more.—<br>Thou art, great lord, my father’s sister’s son,<br>A cousin-german to great Priam’s seed.<br>The obligation of our [[motifs/blood\|blood]] forbids<br>A gory emulation ‘twixt us twain.<br>Were thy commixtion Greek and Trojan so<br>That thou couldst say ’This hand is Grecian all,<br>And this is Trojan; the sinews of this leg<br>All Greek, and this all Troy; my mother’s [[motifs/blood\|blood]]<br>Runs on the dexter cheek, and this sinister<br>Bounds in my father‘s,’ by Jove multipotent<br>Thou shouldst not bear from me a Greekish member<br>Wherein my sword had not impressure made<br>Of our rank feud. But the just gods gainsay<br>That any drop thou borrowed’st from thy mother,<br>My sacred aunt, should by my mortal sword<br>Be drained. Let me embrace thee, [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]].<br>By him that thunders, thou hast lusty arms.<br>[[characters/hector\|Hector]] would have them fall upon him thus.<br>Cousin, all [[concepts/honour\|honour]] to thee. |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | I thank thee, [[characters/hector\|Hector]].<br>Thou art too gentle and too free a man.<br>I came to kill thee, cousin, and bear hence<br>A great addition earned in thy [[motifs/death\|death]]. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Not Neoptolemus so mirable,<br>On whose bright crest Fame with her loud‘st oyez<br>Cries ’This is he!’, could promise to himself<br>A thought of added [[concepts/honour\|honour]] torn from [[characters/hector\|Hector]]. |
| AENEAS | There is expectance here from both the sides<br>What further you will do. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | We’ll answer it:<br>The issue is embracement.—[[characters/ajax\|Ajax]], farewell. |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | If I might in entreaties find success,<br>As seld I have the chance, I would [[motifs/desire\|desire]]<br>My famous cousin to our Grecian tents. |
| DIOMEDES | ’Tis [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]]’s wish—and great [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]]<br>Doth long to see unarmed the valiant [[characters/hector\|Hector]]. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Aeneas, call my brother [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] to me,<br>And signify this loving interview<br>To the expecters of our Trojan part. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[motifs/desire\|Desire]] them [[motifs/home\|home]]. Exit Aeneas |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Give me thy hand, my cousin.<br>I will go eat with thee, and see your knights. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]] and the rest: Aeneas, [[characters/ulysses\|Ulysses]], |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Menelaus, Nestor, [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]], Patroclus, [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]], and<br><br>others |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | Great [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]] comes to meet us here. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | (to Aeneas)<br>The worthiest of them, tell me name by name.<br>But for [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]], mine own searching eyes<br>Shall find him by his large and portly size. |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | (embracing him)<br>Worthy of arms, as welcome as to one<br>That would be rid of such an enemy.<br>But that’s no welcome. Understand more clear:<br>What’s past and what’s to come is strewed with husks<br>And formless ruin of oblivion,<br>But in this extant moment [[concepts/faith\|faith]] and troth,<br>Strained purely from all hollow bias-drawing,<br>Bids thee with most divine integrity<br>From heart of very heart, ‘Great [[characters/hector\|Hector]], welcome!’ |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | I thank thee, most imperious [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] to [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | My [[motifs/well\|well]]-famed lord of Troy, no less to you. |
| MENELAUS | Let me confirm my princely brother’s greeting.<br>You brace of warlike brothers, welcome hither. |
| *(didascalia)* | He embraces [[characters/hector\|Hector]] and [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | (to Aeneas)<br>Who must we answer? |
| AENEAS | The noble Menelaus. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | O, you, my lord! By Mars his gauntlet, thanks.<br>Mock not that I affect th’untraded oath.<br>Your quondam wife swears still by [[characters/venus\|Venus]]’ glove.<br>She’s [[motifs/well\|well]], but bade me not commend her to you. |
| MENELAUS | Name her not now, sir. She’s a deadly theme. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | O, pardon. I offend. |
| NESTOR | I have, thou gallant Trojan, seen thee oft,<br>Labouring for destiny, make cruel way<br>Through ranks of Greekish youth, and I have seen thee<br>As hot as Perseus spur thy Phrygian steed,<br>And seen thee scorning forfeits and subduements,<br>When thou hast hung th‘advancèd sword i’th’ air,<br>Not letting it decline on the declined,<br>That I have said unto my standers-by,<br>‘Lo, Jupiter is yonder, dealing life’.<br>And I have seen thee pause and take thy breath,<br>When that a ring of Greeks have hemmed thee in,<br>Like an Olympian, wrestling. This have I seen;<br>But this thy countenance, still locked in steel,<br>I never saw till now. I knew thy grandsire<br>And once fought with him. He was a soldier good,<br>But—by great Mars, the captain of us all—<br>Never like thee. Let an old man embrace thee;<br>And, worthy warrior, welcome to our tents.<br>He embraces [[characters/hector\|Hector]] |
| AENEAS | (to [[characters/hector\|Hector]]) ’Tis the old Nestor. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Let me embrace thee, good old chronicle,<br>That hast so long walked hand in hand with [[motifs/time\|time]].<br>Most reverend Nestor, I am glad to clasp thee. |
| NESTOR | I would my arms could match thee in contention<br>As they contend with thee in courtesy. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | I would they could. |
| NESTOR | Ha! By this white beard I’d fight with thee tomorrow.<br>[[motifs/well\|Well]], welcome, welcome! I have seen the [[motifs/time\|time]]. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | I [[concepts/wonder\|wonder]] now how yonder [[motifs/city\|city]] stands<br>When we have here her base and pillar by us? |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | I know your favour, Lord [[characters/ulysses\|Ulysses]], [[motifs/well\|well]].<br>Ah, sir, there’s many a Greek and Trojan dead<br>Since first I saw yourself and Diomed<br>In Ilium on your Greekish embassy. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | Sir, I foretold you then what would ensue.<br>My prophecy is but half his [[motifs/journey\|journey]] yet;<br>For yonder walls that pertly front your town,<br>Yon towers whose wanton tops do buss the clouds,<br>Must kiss their own feet. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | I must not believe you.<br>There they stand yet, and modestly I think<br>The fall of every Phrygian stone will cost<br>A drop of Grecian [[motifs/blood\|blood]]. The end crowns all,<br>And that old common arbitrator [[motifs/time\|Time]]<br>Will one day end it. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | So to him we leave it.<br>Most gentle and most valiant [[characters/hector\|Hector]], welcome. |
| *(didascalia)* | He embraces him |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | After the General, I beseech you next<br>To feast with me and see me at my tent. |
| *(didascalia)* | I shall forestall thee, Lord [[characters/ulysses\|Ulysses]]. To [[characters/hector\|Hector]] Thou! |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | Now, [[characters/hector\|Hector]], I have fed mine eyes on thee.<br>I have with exact view perused thee, [[characters/hector\|Hector]],<br>And quoted joint by joint. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Is this [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]]? |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | I am [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]]. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Stand fair, I pray thee, let me look on thee. |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | Behold thy fill. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Nay, I have done already. |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | Thou art too brief. I will the second [[motifs/time\|time]],<br>As I would buy thee, view thee limb by limb. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | O, like a book of sport thou‘lt read me o’er.<br>But there’s more in me than thou understand’st.<br>Why dost thou so oppress me with thine eye? |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | Tell me, you heavens, in which part of his [[concepts/body\|body]]<br>Shall I destroy him—whether there, or there, or<br>there—<br>That I may give the local wound a name,<br>And make distinct the very breach whereout<br>[[characters/hector\|Hector]]’s great spirit flew? Answer me, heavens. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | It would discredit the blest gods, proud man,<br>To answer such a question. Stand again.<br>Think’st thou to catch my life so pleasantly<br>As to prenominate in nice conjecture<br>Where thou wilt hit me dead? |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | I tell thee, yea. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Wert thou the oracle to tell me so,<br>I’d not believe thee. Henceforth guard thee [[motifs/well\|well]].<br>For I’ll not kill thee there, nor there, nor there,<br>But, by the forge that stithied Mars his helm,<br>I’ll kill thee everywhere, yea, o‘er and o’er.—<br>You wisest Grecians, pardon me this brag:<br>His insolence draws folly from my lips.<br>But I’ll endeavour deeds to match these words,<br>Or may I never— |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | Do not chafe thee, cousin.—<br>And you, [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]], let these threats alone,<br>Till accident or purpose bring you to’t.<br>You may have every day enough of [[characters/hector\|Hector]],<br>If you have stomach. The general state, I [[concepts/fear\|fear]],<br>Can scarce entreat you to be odd with him. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | (to [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]])<br>I pray you, let us see you in the field.<br>We have had pelting wars since you refused<br>The Grecians’ cause. |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | Dost thou entreat me, [[characters/hector\|Hector]]?<br>Tomorrow do I meet thee, fell as [[motifs/death\|death]];<br>Tonight, all friends. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Thy hand upon that match. |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | First, all you peers of Greece, go to my tent.<br>There in the full convive you. Afterwards,<br>As [[characters/hector\|Hector]]’s leisure and your bounties shall<br>Concur together, severally entreat him.<br>Beat loud the taborins, let the trumpets blow,<br>That this great soldier may his welcome know. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Exeunt all but [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] and [[characters/ulysses\|Ulysses]] |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | My Lord [[characters/ulysses\|Ulysses]], tell me, I beseech you,<br>In what place of the field doth Calchas keep? |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | At Menelaus’ tent, most princely [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]].<br>There Diomed doth feast with him tonight—<br>Who neither looks on heaven nor on earth,<br>But gives all gaze and bent of amorous view<br>On the fair Cressid. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Shall I, sweet lord, be bound to you so much,<br>After we part from [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]]’s tent,<br>To bring me thither? |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | You shall command me, sir.<br>As gentle tell me, of what [[concepts/honour\|honour]] was<br>This [[characters/cressida\|Cressida]] in Troy? Had she no [[archetypes/lover\|lover]] there<br>That wails her absence? |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | O sir, to such as boasting show their scars<br>A mock is due. Will you walk on, my lord?<br>She was beloved, she loved; she is, and doth.<br>But still sweet [[motifs/love\|love]] is food for fortune’s tooth. Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]] and Patroclus |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | I’ll heat his [[motifs/blood\|blood]] with Greekish wine tonight,<br>Which with my scimitar I’ll cool tomorrow.<br>Patroclus, let us feast him to the height. |
| PATROCLUS | Here comes [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]] |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | How now, thou core of envy,<br>Thou crusty botch of [[concepts/nature\|nature]], what’s the news? |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Why, thou picture of what thou seemest, and idol of idiot-worshippers, here’s a letter for thee. |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | From whence, [[forms/fragment\|fragment]]? |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Why, thou full dish of [[archetypes/fool\|fool]], from Troy.<br>[[characters/achilles\|Achilles]] reads the letter |
| PATROCLUS | Who keeps the tent now? |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | The surgeon’s box or the patient’s wound. |
| PATROCLUS | [[motifs/well\|Well]] said, adversity. And what need these tricks? |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Prithee be silent, boy. I profit not by thy talk.<br>Thou art thought to be [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]]’ male varlet. |
| PATROCLUS | ‘Male varlet’, you rogue? What’s that? |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Why, his masculine whore. Now the rotten diseases of the south, guts-griping, ruptures, catarrhs, loads o’ gravel i’th’ back, lethargies, cold palsies, and the like, take and take again such preposterous discoveries! |
| PATROCLUS | Why, thou damnable box of envy thou, what mean’st thou to curse thus? |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Do I curse thee? |
| PATROCLUS | Why, no, you ruinous butt, you whoreson indistinguishable cur, no. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | No? Why art thou then exasperate? Thou idle immaterial skein of sleave-silk, thou green sarsenet flap for a sore eye, thou tassel of a prodigal’s purse, thou! Ah, how the poor world is pestered with such waterflies! Diminutives of [[concepts/nature\|nature]]. |
| PATROCLUS | Out, gall! |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Finch egg! |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | My sweet Patroclus, I am thwarted quite<br>From my great purpose in tomorrow’s battle.<br>Here is a letter from [[characters/queen\|Queen]] Hecuba,<br>A token from her daughter, my fair [[motifs/love\|love]],<br>Both taxing me, and gaging me to keep<br>An oath that I have sworn. I will not break it.<br>Fall, Greeks; fail, fame; [[concepts/honour\|honour]], or go or stay.<br>My major vow lies here; this I’ll obey.—<br>Come, come, [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]], help to trim my tent.<br>This [[motifs/night\|night]] in banqueting must all be spent.—<br>Away, Patroclus. Exeunt [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]] and Patroclus |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | With too much [[motifs/blood\|blood]] and too little brain these two may run mad, but if with too much brain and too little [[motifs/blood\|blood]] they do, I’ll be a curer of madmen. Here’s [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]]: an honest fellow enough, and one that loves quails, but he has not so much brain as ear-wax. And the goodly transformation of Jupiter there, his brother the bull, the primitive statue and oblique memorial of cuckolds, a thrifty shoeing-horn in a chain, hanging at his brother’s leg: to what form but that he is should wit larded with malice and malice farced with wit turn him to? To an ass were nothing: he is both ass and ox. To an ox were nothing: he is both ox and ass. To be a dog, a mule, a cat, a fitchew, a toad, a lizard, an owl, a puttock, or a herring without a roe, I would not care; but to be Menetaus!—I would conspire against destiny. Ask me not what I would be if I were not [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]], for I care not to be the louse of a lazar, so I were not Menetaus.—Hey-day, sprites and fires. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/hector\|Hector]], [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]], [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]], [[characters/ulysses\|Ulysses]], Nestor, |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Menelaus, [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]], and Diomedes, with lights |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | We go wrong, we go wrong. |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | No, yonder ’tis:<br>There, where we see the light. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | I trouble you. |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | No, not a whit. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]] |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | Here comes himself to guide you. |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | Welcome, brave [[characters/hector\|Hector]]. Welcome, princes all. |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | (to [[characters/hector\|Hector]])<br>So now, fair prince of Troy, I bid good [[motifs/night\|night]].<br>[[characters/ajax\|Ajax]] commands the guard to tend on you. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Thanks and good [[motifs/night\|night]] to the Greeks’ general. |
| MENELAUS | Good [[motifs/night\|night]], my lord. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Good [[motifs/night\|night]], sweet Lord Menelaus. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | (aside) Sweet draught! ‘Sweet’, quoth a? Sweet sink, sweet sewer. |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | Good [[motifs/night\|night]] and welcome both at once, to those<br>That go or tarry. |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | Good [[motifs/night\|night]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]] and Menelaus |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | Old Nestor tarries, and you too, Diomed.<br>Keep [[characters/hector\|Hector]] company an hour or two. |
| DIOMEDES | I cannot, lord. I have important business<br>The tide whereof is now.—Good [[motifs/night\|night]], great [[characters/hector\|Hector]]. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Give me your hand. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | (aside to [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]])<br>Follow his torch, he goes to Calchas’ tent.<br>I’ll keep you company. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | (aside) Sweet sir, you [[concepts/honour\|honour]] me. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | (to Diomedes)<br>And so good [[motifs/night\|night]]. |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | Come, come, enter my tent. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Diomedes, followed by [[characters/ulysses\|Ulysses]] and |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]], at one door; and [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]], [[characters/hector\|Hector]],<br>[[characters/ajax\|Ajax]], and Nestor at another door |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | That same Diomed’s a false-hearted rogue, a most unjust knave. I will no more trust him when he leers than I will a serpent when he hisses. He will spend his mouth and promise like Brabbler the hound, but when he performs astronomers foretell it: that is prodigious, there will come some change. The sun borrows of the [[motifs/moon\|moon]] when Diomed keeps his word. I will rather leave to see [[characters/hector\|Hector]] than not to dog him. They say he keeps a Trojan drab, and uses the traitor Calchas his tent. I’ll after.—Nothing but lechery! All incontinent varlets! Exit |

### Atto 5, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Diomedes |
| DIOMEDES | What, are you up here? Ho! Speak! |
| *(didascalia)* | CALCHAS at the door Who calls? |
| DIOMEDES | Diomed. Calchas, I think. Where’s your daughter? |
| *(didascalia)* | CALCHAS at the door She comes to you. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] and [[characters/ulysses\|Ulysses]], unseen |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | (aside)<br>Stand where the torch may not discover us. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | (aside)<br>Cressid comes forth to him. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/cressida\|Cressida]] |
| DIOMEDES | How now, my charge? |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Now, my sweet guardian. Hark, a word with you.<br>She whispers to him. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]], unseen |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | (aside) Yea, so familiar? |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | (aside) She will sing any man at first sight. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | (aside) And any man may sing her, if he can take her clef. She’s noted. |
| DIOMEDES | Will you remember? |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Remember? Yes. |
| DIOMEDES | Nay, but do then, And let your mind be coupled with your words. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | (aside) What should she remember? |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | (aside) List! |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Sweet honey Greek, tempt me no more to folly. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | (aside) Roguery. |
| DIOMEDES | Nay, then! |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | I’ll tell you what— |
| DIOMEDES | Fo, fo! Come, tell a pin. You are forsworn. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | In [[concepts/faith\|faith]], I cannot. What would you have me do? |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | (aside) A juggling trick: to be secretly open. |
| DIOMEDES | What did you swear you would bestow on me? |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | I prithee, do not hold me to mine oath.<br>Bid me do anything but that, sweet Greek. |
| DIOMEDES | Good [[motifs/night\|night]]. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | (aside)<br>Hold, patience! |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | (aside) How now, Trojan? |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Diomed. |
| DIOMEDES | No, no, good [[motifs/night\|night]]. I’ll be your [[archetypes/fool\|fool]] no more. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | (aside) Thy better must. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Hark, one word in your ear.<br>She whispers to him |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | (aside) O plague and [[concepts/madness\|madness]]! |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | (aside)<br>You are moved, Prince. Let us depart, I pray you,<br>Lest your displeasure should enlarge itself<br>To wrathful terms. This place is dangerous,<br>The [[motifs/time\|time]] right deadly. I beseech you go. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | (aside)<br>Behold, I pray you. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | (aside) Nay, good my lord, go off.<br>You flow to great distraction. Come, my lord. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | (aside)<br>I prithee, stay. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | (aside) You have not patience. Come. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | (aside)<br>I pray you, stay. By hell and all hell’s torments,<br>I will not speak a word. |
| DIOMEDES | And so good [[motifs/night\|night]]. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Nay, but you part in anger. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | (aside) Doth that grieve thee?<br>O withered [[concepts/truth\|truth]]! |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | (aside) Why, how now, lord? |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | (aside) By Jove,<br>I will be patient. |
| *(didascalia)* | Diomedes starts to go |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Guardian! Why, Greek! |
| DIOMEDES | Fo, fo! Adieu. You palter. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | In [[concepts/faith\|faith]], I do not. Come hither once again. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | (aside)<br>You shake, my lord, at something. Will you go?<br>You will break out. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | (aside) She strokes his cheek. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | (aside) Come, come. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | (aside)<br>Nay, stay. By Jove, I will not speak a word.<br>There is between my will and all offences<br>A guard of patience. Stay a little while. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | (aside) How the devil Luxury with his fat rump and potato finger tickles these together! Fry, lechery, fry. |
| DIOMEDES | But will you then? |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | In [[concepts/faith\|faith]], I will, la. Never trust me else. |
| DIOMEDES | Give me some token for the surety of it. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | I’ll fetch you one. Exit |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | (aside) You have sworn patience. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | (aside) [[concepts/fear\|Fear]] me not, sweet lord.<br>I will not be myself, nor have cognition<br>Of what I feel. I am all patience. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/cressida\|Cressida]] with [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]]’ sleeve |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | (aside) Now the pledge! Now, now, now. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Here Diomed, keep this sleeve. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | (aside) O [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]], where is thy [[concepts/faith\|faith]]? |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | (aside) My lord. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | (aside)<br>I will be patient; outwardly I will. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | You look upon that sleeve. Behold it [[motifs/well\|well]].<br>He loved me—O false wench!—give’t me again.<br>She takes it back |
| DIOMEDES | Whose was’t? |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | It is no matter, now I ha’t again.<br>I will not meet with you tomorrow [[motifs/night\|night]].<br>I prithee, Diomed, visit me no more. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | (aside) Now she sharpens. [[motifs/well\|Well]] said, whetstone. |
| DIOMEDES | I shall have it. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | What, this? |
| DIOMEDES | Ay, that. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | O all you gods! O pretty pretty pledge!<br>Thy master now lies thinking on his bed<br>Of thee and me, and sighs, and takes my glove<br>And gives memorial dainty kisses to it— |
| *(didascalia)* | DIOMEDES |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | As I kiss thee. |
| *(didascalia)* | he snatches the sieeve |
| *(didascalia)* | [[characters/cressida\|CRESSiDA]] Nay, do not snatch it from me. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | He that takes that doth take my heart withal. |
| DIOMEDES | I had your heart before; this follows it. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | (aside) I did swear patience. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | You shall not have it, Diomed. [[concepts/faith\|Faith]], you shall not.<br>I’ll give you something else. |
| DIOMEDES | I will have this. Whose was it? |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | It is no matter. |
| DIOMEDES | Come, tell me whose it was? |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | ’Twas one’s that loved me better than you will.<br>But now you have it, take it. |
| DIOMEDES | Whose was it? |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | By all [[characters/diana\|Diana]]’s waiting-women yond,<br>And by herself, I will not tell you whose. |
| DIOMEDES | Tomorrow will I wear it on my helm,<br>And grieve his spirit that dares, not challenge it. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | (aside)<br>Wert thou the devil and wor’st it on thy horn,<br>It should be challenged. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], [[motifs/well\|well]], ‘tis done, ’tis past—and yet it is not.<br>I will not keep my word. |
| DIOMEDES | Why then, farewell.<br>Thou never shalt mock Diomed again. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | You shall not go. One cannot speak a word<br>But it straight starts you. |
| DIOMEDES | I do not like this fooling. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] (aside) |
| DIOMEDES | Nor I, by Pluto—but that that likes not you<br>Pleases me best. |
| DIOMEDES | What, shall I come? The hour— |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Ay, come. O Jove, do come. I shall be plagued. |
| DIOMEDES | Farewell till then. |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | Good [[motifs/night\|night]]. I prithee, come. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Diomedes |
| [[characters/cressida\|CRESSIDA]] | [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]], farewell. One eye yet looks on thee,<br>But with my heart the other eye doth see.<br>Ah, poor our sex! This fault in us I find:<br>The error of our eye directs our mind.<br>What error leads must err. O then conclude:<br>Minds swayed by eyes are full of turpitude. Exit |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | (aside)<br>A proof of strength she could not publish more<br>Unless she said, ‘My mind is now turned whore’. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | All’s done, my lord. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | It is. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | Why stay we then? |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | To make a recordation to my [[motifs/soul\|soul]]<br>Of every syllable that here was spoke.<br>But if I tell how these two did co-act,<br>Shall I not lie in publishing a [[concepts/truth\|truth]]?<br>Sith yet there is a credence in my heart,<br>An esperance so obstinately strong,<br>That doth invert th’attest of eyes and ears,<br>As if those organs had deceptious functions<br>Created only to calumniate.<br>Was Cressid here? |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | I cannot conjure, Trojan. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | She was not, sure. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | Most sure, she was. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Why, my negation hath no taste of [[concepts/madness\|madness]]. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | Nor mine, my lord. Cressid was here but now. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Let it not be believed, for womanhood.<br>Think: we had mothers. Do not give advantage<br>To stubborn critics, apt without a theme<br>For depravation to square the general sex<br>By Cressid’s rule. Rather, think this not Cressid. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | What hath she done, Prince, that can soil our mothers? |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Nothing at all, unless that this were she. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | (aside) Will a swagger himself out on’s own eyes? |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | This, she? No, this is Diomed’s [[characters/cressida\|Cressida]].<br>If [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]] have a [[motifs/soul\|soul]], this is not she.<br>If souls guide vows, if vows be sanctimonies,<br>If sanctimony be the gods’ delight,<br>If there be rule in unity itself,<br>This is not she. O [[concepts/madness\|madness]] of discourse,<br>That cause sets up with and against thyselfl<br>Bifold authority, where reason can revolt<br>Without perdition, and loss assume all reason<br>Without revolt! This is and is not Cressid. |
| *(didascalia)* | Within my [[motifs/soul\|soul]] there doth conduce a fight |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Of this strange [[concepts/nature\|nature]], that a thing inseparate<br>Divides more wider than the sky and earth,<br>And yet the spacious breadth of this division<br>Admits no orifex for a point as subtle<br>As Ariachne’s broken woof to enter.<br>Instance, O instance, strong as Pluto’s gates:<br>Cressid is mine, tied with the bonds of heaven.<br>Instance, O instance, strong as heaven itself:<br>The bonds of heaven are slipped, dissolved, and loosed,<br>And with another knot, five-finger-tied,<br>The fractions of her [[concepts/faith\|faith]], orts of her [[motifs/love\|love]],<br>The fragments, scraps, the bits and greasy relics<br>Of her o’er-eaten [[concepts/faith\|faith]], are bound to Diomed. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | May worthy [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] e’en be half attached<br>With that which here his passion doth express? |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Ay, Greek, and that shall be divulged [[motifs/well\|well]]<br>In characters as red as Mars his heart<br>Inflamed with [[characters/venus\|Venus]]. Never did young man fancy<br>With so eternal and so fixed a [[motifs/soul\|soul]].<br>Hark, Greek: as much as I do Cressid [[motifs/love\|love]],<br>So much by weight hate I her Diomed.<br>That sleeve is mine that he’ll bear in his helm.<br>Were it a casque composed by Vulcan’s skill,<br>My sword should bite it. Not the dreadful spout<br>Which shipmen do the hurricano call,<br>Constringed in mass by the almighty sun,<br>Shall dizzy with more clamour Neptune’s ear<br>In his descent, than shall my prompted sword<br>Falling on Diomed. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | (aside) He’ll tickle it for his concupy. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | O Cressid, O false Cressid! False, false, false.<br>Let all untruths stand by thy stained name,<br>And they’ll seem glorious. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | O contain yourself.<br>Your passion draws ears hither. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Aeneas |
| AENEAS | (to [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]])<br>I have been seeking you this hour, my lord.<br>[[characters/hector\|Hector]] by this is arming him in Troy.<br>[[characters/ajax\|Ajax]] your guard stays to conduct you [[motifs/home\|home]]. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Have with you, Prince.—My courteous lord, adieu.—<br>Farewell, revolted fair; and Diomed,<br>Stand fast and wear a [[settings/castle\|castle]] on thy head. |
| [[characters/ulysses\|ULYSSES]] | I’ll bring you to the gates. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Accept distracted thanks. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]], Aeneas, and [[characters/ulysses\|Ulysses]] |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Would I could meet that rogue Diomed! I would croak like a raven. I would bode, I would bode. Patroclus will give me anything for the intelligence of this whore. The parrot will not do more for an almond than he for a commodious drab. Lechery, lechery, still wars and lechery! Nothing else holds fashion. A burning devil take them! Exit |

### Atto 5, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/hector\|Hector]] armed, and Andromache |
| ANDROMACHE | When was my lord so much ungently tempered<br>To stop his ears against admonishment?<br>Unarm, unarm, and do not fight today. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | You train me to offend you. Get you in.<br>By all the everlasting gods, I’ll go. |
| ANDROMACHE | My [[motifs/dreams\|dreams]] will sure prove ominous to the day. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | No more, I say. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Cassandra |
| CASSANDRA | Where is my brother [[characters/hector\|Hector]]? |
| ANDROMACHE | Here, sister, armed and bloody in intent.<br>Consort with me in loud and dear petition,<br>Pursue we him on knees—for I have dreamed<br>Of bloody turbulence, and this whole [[motifs/night\|night]]<br>Hath nothing been but shapes and forms of slaughter. |
| CASSANDRA | O ’tis true. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Ho! Bid my trumpet sound. |
| CASSANDRA | No notes of sally, for the heavens, sweet brother. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Begone, I say. The gods have heard me swear. |
| CASSANDRA | The gods are deaf to hot and peevish vows.<br>They are polluted off’rings, more abhorred<br>Than spotted livers in the sacrifice. |
| ANDROMACHE | (to [[characters/hector\|Hector]])<br>O, be persuaded. Do not count it holy<br>To hurt by being just. It is as lawful,<br>For we would give much, to use violent thefts,<br>And rob in the behalf of [[concepts/charity\|charity]]. |
| CASSANDRA | It is the purpose that makes strong the vow,<br>But vows to every purpose must not hold.<br>Unarm, sweet [[characters/hector\|Hector]]. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Hold you still, I say.<br>Mine [[concepts/honour\|honour]] keeps the weather of my fate.<br>Life every man holds dear, but the dear man<br>Holds [[concepts/honour\|honour]] far more precious-dear than life. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]], armed |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | How now, young man, mean’st thou to fight today? |
| *(didascalia)* | ANDROMACHE aside |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Cassandra, call my father to persuade. Exit Cassandra |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | No, [[concepts/faith\|faith]], young [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]]. Doff thy harness, youth.<br>I am today i’th’ vein of chivalry.<br>Let grow thy sinews till their knots be strong,<br>And tempt not yet the brushes of the war.<br>Unarm thee, go—and [[concepts/doubt\|doubt]] thou not, brave boy,<br>I’ll stand today for thee and me and Troy. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Brother, you have a vice of [[concepts/mercy\|mercy]] in you,<br>Which better fits a [[motifs/lion\|lion]] than a man. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | What vice is that? Good [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]], chide me for it. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | When many times the captive Grecian falls<br>Even in the fan and [[motifs/wind\|wind]] of your fair sword,<br>You bid them rise and live. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | O ’tis fair play. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | [[archetypes/fool\|Fool]]’s play, by heaven, [[characters/hector\|Hector]]. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | How now! How now! |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | For th’ [[motifs/love\|love]] of all the gods,<br>Let’s leave the hermit pity with our mother<br>And, when we have our armours buckled on,<br>The venomed vengeance ride upon our swords,<br>Spur them to ruthful work, rein them from ruth. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Fie, savage, fie! |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | [[characters/hector\|Hector]], then ’tis wars. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]], I would not have you fight today. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Who should withhold me?<br>Not fate, obedience, nor the hand of Mars<br>Beck‘ning with fiery truncheon my retire,<br>Not Priamus and Hecuba on knees,<br>Their eyes o’er-gallèd with recourse of [[motifs/tears\|tears]],<br>Nor you, my brother, with your true sword drawn<br>Opposed to hinder me, should stop my way<br>But by my ruin. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Priam and Cassandra |
| CASSANDRA | Lay hold upon him, Priam, hold him fast.<br>He is thy crutch: now if thou loose thy stay,<br>Thou on him leaning and all Troy on thee,<br>Fall all together. |
| PRIAM | Come, [[characters/hector\|Hector]], come. Go back.<br>Thy wife hath dreamt, thy mother hath had visions,<br>Cassandra doth foresee, and I myself<br>Am like a [[archetypes/prophet\|prophet]] suddenly enrapt<br>To tell thee that this day is ominous.<br>Therefore come back. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Aeneas is afield,<br>And I do stand engaged to many Greeks,<br>Even in the [[concepts/faith\|faith]] of valour, to appear<br>This morning to them. |
| PRIAM | Ay, but thou shalt not go. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] kneeling I must not break my [[concepts/faith\|faith]]. |
| PRIAM | You know me dutiful; therefore, dear sire,<br>Let me not shame respect, but give me leave<br>To take that course, by your consent and voice,<br>Which you do here forbid me, royal Priam. |
| CASSANDRA | O Priam, yield not to him. |
| ANDROMACHE | Do not, dear father. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | Andromache, I am offended with you.<br>Upon the [[motifs/love\|love]] you bear me, get you in. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Andromache |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | This foolish, dreaming, superstitious girl<br>Makes all these bodements. |
| CASSANDRA | O farewell, dear [[characters/hector\|Hector]].<br>Look how thou diest; look how thy eye turns pale;<br>Look how thy wounds do bleed at many vents.<br>Hark how Troy roars, how Hecuba cries out,<br>How poor Andromache shrills her dolours forth.<br>Behold: distraction, frenzy, and amazement<br>Like witless antics one another meet,<br>And all cry ‘[[characters/hector\|Hector]], [[characters/hector\|Hector]]’s dead, O [[characters/hector\|Hector]]!’ |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Away, away! |
| CASSANDRA | Farewell. Yet soft: [[characters/hector\|Hector]], I take my leave.<br>Thou dost thyself and all our Troy deceive. Exit |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | (to Priam)<br>You are amazed, my liege, at her exclaim.<br>Go in and cheer the town. We’ll forth and fight,<br>Do deeds of praise, and tell you them at [[motifs/night\|night]]. |
| PRIAM | Farewell. The gods with safety stand about thee. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Priam and [[characters/hector\|Hector]] severally. Alarum |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | They are at it, hark! Proud Diomed, believe<br>I come to lose my arm or win my sleeve. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/pandarus\|Pandarus]] |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Do you hear, my lord, do you hear? |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | What now? |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Here’s a letter come from yon poor girl. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Let me read.<br>[[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] reads the letter |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | A whoreson phthisic, a whoreson rascally phthisic so troubles me, and the foolish fortune of this girl, and what one thing, what another, that I shall leave you one o’ these days. And I have a rheum in mine eyes too, and such an ache in my bones that unless a man were cursed I cannot tell what to think on’t.—What says she there? no |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | (tearing the letter)<br>Words, words, mere words, no matter from the heart.<br>Th’effect doth operate another way.<br>Go, [[motifs/wind\|wind]], to [[motifs/wind\|wind]]: there turn and change together.<br>My [[motifs/love\|love]] with words and errors still she feeds,<br>But edifies another with her deeds. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | Why, but hear you— |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Hence, broker-lackey! Ignomy and shame<br>Pursue thy life, and live aye with thy name. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt severally |

### Atto 5, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Alarum. Enter [[characters/thersites\|Thersites]] in excursions |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Now they are clapper-clawing one another. I’ll go look on. That dissembling abominable varlet Diomed has got that same scurvy doting foolish young knave’s sleeve of Troy there in his helm. I would fain see them meet, that that same young Trojan ass that loves the whore there might send that Greekish whoremasterly [[archetypes/villain\|villain]] with the sleeve back to the dissembling luxurious drab of a sleeveless errand. O‘th’ t’other side, the policy of those crafty swearing rascals—that stale old mouse-eaten dry cheese Nestor and that same dog-fox [[characters/ulysses\|Ulysses]]—is proved not worth a blackberry. They set me up in policy that mongrel cur [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]] against that dog of as bad a kind [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]]. And now is the cur [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]] prouder than the cur [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]], and will not arm today—whereupon the Grecians began to proclaim barbarism, and policy grows into an ill opinion. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Diomedes, followed by [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Soft, here comes sleeve and t’other. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | (to Diomedes)<br>Fly not, for shouldst thou take the [[motifs/river\|river]] Styx<br>I would swim after. |
| DIOMEDES | Thou dost miscall retire.<br>I do not fly, but advantageous care<br>Withdrew me from the odds of multitude. Have at<br>thee!<br>They fight |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Hold thy whore, Grecian! Now for thy whore, Trojan! Now the sleeve, now the sleeve! |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Diomedes driving in [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/hector\|Hector]] behind |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | What art thou, Greek? Art thou for [[characters/hector\|Hector]]’s match?<br>Art thou of [[motifs/blood\|blood]] and [[concepts/honour\|honour]]? |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | No, no, I am a rascal, a scurvy railing knave, a very filthy rogue. |
| [[characters/hector\|HECTOR]] | I do believe thee: live. |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | God-a-[[concepts/mercy\|mercy]], that thou wilt believe me— |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit [[characters/hector\|Hector]] |
| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | but a plague break thy neck for frighting me. What’s become of the wenching rogues? I think they have swallowed one another. I would laugh at that miracle—yet in a sort lechery eats itself. I’ll seek them. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |

### Atto 5, Scena 5

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Diomedes and Servants |
| DIOMEDES | Go, go, my servant, take thou [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]]’ [[motifs/horse\|horse]].<br>Present the fair steed to my Lady Cressid.<br>Fellow, commend my service to her [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]].<br>Tell her I have chastised the amorous Trojan,<br>And am her knight by proof. |
| SERVANT | I go, my lord. Exit |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]] |
| [[characters/agamemnon\|AGAMEMNON]] | Renew, renew! The fierce Polydamas<br>Hath beat down Menon; bastard Margareton<br>Hath Doreus prisoner,<br>And stands colossus-wise waving his beam<br>Upon the pashèd corpses of the kings<br>Epistropus and Cedius; [[characters/polixenes\|Polixenes]] is slain,<br>Amphimacus and Thoas deadly hurt,<br>Patroclus ta’en or slain, and Palamedes<br>Sore hurt and bruised; the dreadful sagittary<br>Appals our numbers. Haste we, Diomed,<br>To reinforcement, or we perish all. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Nestor |
| *(didascalia)* | with Patroclus’ [[concepts/body\|body]] |
| NESTOR | Go, bear Patroclus’ [[concepts/body\|body]] to [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]],<br>And bid the snail-paced [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]] arm for shame. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit one or more with the [[concepts/body\|body]] |
| NESTOR | There is a thousand Hectors in the field.<br>Now here he fights on Galathe his [[motifs/horse\|horse]],<br>And there lacks work; anon he’s there afoot,<br>And there they fly or die, like scalèd schools<br>Before the belching whale. Then is he yonder,<br>And there the strawy Greeks, ripe for his edge,<br>Fall down before him like the mower’s swath.<br>Here, there, and everywhere he [[motifs/leaves\|leaves]] and takes,<br>Dexterity so obeying appetite<br>That what he will he does, and does so much<br>That proof is called impossibility. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/ulysses\|Ulysses]] |

Great [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]]<br>Is arming, weeping, cursing, vowing vengeance.<br>Patroclus’ wounds have roused his drowsy [[motifs/blood\|blood]],<br>Together with his mangled Myrmidons,<br>That noseless, handless, hacked and chipped come to<br>him<br>Crying on [[characters/hector\|Hector]]. [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]] hath lost a friend<br>And foams at mouth, and he is armed and at it,<br>Roaring for Troitus—who hath done today<br>Mad and fantastic execution,<br>Engaging and redeeming of himself<br>With such a careless force and forceless care<br>As if that luck, in very spite of cunning,<br>Bade him win all. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/ajax\|Ajax]] |
| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]], thou coward Troitus! Exit |
| *(didascalia)* | DIOMEDES Ay, there, there! Exit |
| NESTOR | So, so, we draw together. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]] |
| [[characters/achilles\|ACHILLES]] | Where is this [[characters/hector\|Hector]]?<br>Come, come, thou brave boy-queller, show thy face.<br>Know what it is to meet [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]] angry.<br>[[characters/hector\|Hector]]! Where’s [[characters/hector\|Hector]]? I will none but [[characters/hector\|Hector]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | Were I the general, thou shouldst have my office<br>Ere that correction.—Troitus, I say! What, [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]]! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] |

| [[characters/ajax\|AJAX]] | I’ll fight with him alone. Stand, Diomed. |

| *(didascalia)* | Enter one in sumptuous armour |

| ALL THE OTHERS | [[characters/hector\|Hector]]? The gods forbid. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | He’s dead, and at the murderer’s [[motifs/horse\|horse]]’s tail<br>In beastly sort dragged through the shameful field.<br>Frown on, you heavens; effect your rage with [[characters/speed\|speed]];<br>Sit, gods, upon your thrones, and smite at Troy.<br>I say, at once: let your brief plagues be [[concepts/mercy\|mercy]],<br>And linger not our sure destructions on. |

[[characters/hector\|Hector]] is gone.<br>Who shall tell Priam so, or Hecuba?<br>Let him that will a screech-owl aye be called<br>Go into Troy and say their [[characters/hector\|Hector]]’s dead.<br>There is a word will Priam turn to stone,<br>Make wells and Niobes of the maids and wives,<br>Cold statues of the youth, and in a word<br>Scare Troy out of itself. To Troy with comfort go:<br>[[concepts/hope\|Hope]] of [[concepts/revenge\|revenge]] shall hide our inward woe. |

| [[characters/thersites\|THERSITES]] | Why, his masculine whore. Now the rotten diseases of the south, the guts-griping, ruptures, loads o’ gravel in the back, lethargies, cold palsies, raw eyes, dirt-rotten livers, wheezing lungs, bladders full of impostume, sciaticas, lime-kilns i’th’ palm, incurable bone-ache, and the rivelled fee-simple of the tetter, take and take again such preposterous discoveries.<br>B. The Quarto gives a different ending to the play (which the Folio inadvertently repeats). |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/pandarus\|Pandarus]] |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | But hear you, hear you. |
| *(didascalia)* | Hence, broker-lackey. Strikes him Ignomy and shame |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILUS]] | Pursue thy life, and live aye with thy name. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt all but [[characters/pandarus\|Pandarus]] |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARUS]] | A goodly medicine for my aching bones. O world, world, world!—thus is the poor agent despised. O traitors and bawds, how earnestly are you set a work, and how ill requited! Why should our endeavour be so desired and the performance so loathed? What verse for it? What instance for it? Let me see,<br>Full merrily the humble-bee doth sing<br>Till he hath lost his honey and his sting,<br>And being once subdued in armèd tail,<br>Sweet honey and sweet notes together fail.<br>Good traders in the flesh, set this in your painted cloths:<br>As many as be here of Pandar’s hall,<br>Your eyes, half out, weep out at Pandar’s fall.<br>Or if you cannot weep, yet give some groans,<br>Though not for me, yet for your aching bones.<br>Brethren and sisters of the hold-door trade,<br>Some two months hence my will shall here be made.<br>It should be now, but that my [[concepts/fear\|fear]] is this:<br>Some galled goose of Winchester would hiss.<br>Till then I’ll sweat and seek about for eases,<br>And at that [[motifs/time\|time]] bequeath you my diseases. Exit |
| SONNETS AND | ‘A [[archetypes/lover\|LOVER]]’S COMPLAINT’ |
| SHAKESPEARE’S | Sonnets were published as a collection by Thomas Thorpe in 1609; the title-page declared that they were ‘never before imprinted’. Versions of two of them- 138 and 144—had appeared in 1599, in<br>The Passionate [[archetypes/pilgrim\|Pilgrim]]<br>, a collection ascribed to Shakespeare but including some poems certainly written by other authors; and in the previous year Francis Meres, in<br>Palladis Tamia,<br> had alluded to Shakespeare’s ‘sugared sonnets among his private friends’. The [[forms/sonnet\|sonnet]] sequence had enjoyed a brief but intense vogue from the publication of Sir Philip Sidney’s<br>Astrophil and Stella<br> in 1591 till about 1597. Some of Shakespeare’s plays of this period reflect the fashion: in the [[forms/comedy\|comedy]] of<br>[[motifs/love\|Love]]’s Labour’s Lost<br> the writing of sonnets is seen as a laughable symptom of [[motifs/love\|love]], and in the [[forms/tragedy\|tragedy]] of<br>[[characters/romeo\|Romeo]] and [[characters/juliet\|Juliet]]<br> both speeches of the [[characters/chorus\|Chorus]] and the lovers’ first conversation are in [[forms/sonnet\|sonnet]] form. Later plays use it, too, but it seems likely that most of Shakespeare’s sonnets were first written during this period. But there are indications that some of them were revised; the two printed in<br>The Passionate [[archetypes/pilgrim\|Pilgrim]]<br> differ at certain points from Thorpe’s version, and two other sonnets (2 and 106) exist in manuscript versions which also are not identical with those published in the sequence. We print these as ‘Alternative Versions’ of Sonnets 2, 106, 138, and 144.<br><br>The order in which Thorpe printed the Sonnets has often been questioned, but is not entirely haphazard: all the first seventeen, and no later ones, exhort a young man to marry; all those clearly addressed to one or more men are among the first 126, and all those clearly addressed to, or concerned with, one or more women (the ‘dark lady’) follow. Some of the sonnets in the second group appear to refer to events that prompted sonnets in the first group; it seems likely that the poems were rearranged after composition. Moreover, the volume contains ‘A [[archetypes/lover\|Lover]]’s Complaint’, clearly ascribed to Shakespeare, which stylistic evidence suggests was written in the early seventeenth century and which may have been intended as a companion piece. So, printing the Sonnets in Thorpe’s order, we place them according to the likely date of their revision.<br><br>Textual evidence suggests that Thorpe printed from a transcript by someone other than Shakespeare. His volume bears a dedication over his own initials to ‘Mr W.H.’; we do not know whether this derives from the manuscript, and can only speculate about the dedicatee’s [[concepts/identity\|identity]]. His initials are those of Shakespeare’s only known dedicatee, Henry Wriothesley, Earl of Southampton, but in reverse order. We have even less clue as to the [[concepts/identity\|identity]] of the Sonnets’ other personae, who include a rival poet and a dark woman.<br><br>Shakespeare’s Sonnets may not be autobiographical, but they are certainly unconventional: the most idealistic poems celebrating [[motifs/love\|love]]’s mutuality are addressed by one man to another, and the poems clearly addressed to a woman revile her morals, speak ill of her appearance, and explore the poet’s [[concepts/self\|self]]-disgust at his entanglement with her. The Sonnets include some of the finest [[motifs/love\|love]] poems in the English language: the sequence itself presents an internal drama of great psychological complexity. |
| THESE.ENSUING.SONNETS. |  M<br>r<br>.W.H. ALL.HAPPINESS. |


<span class="qlang-split" data-lang="it"></span>

[[characters/troilus|TROILUS]] AND [[characters/cressida|CRESSIDA]]
[[characters/troilus|Troilus]] and [[characters/cressida|Cressida]], first heard of in a Stationers’ Register entry of 7 February 1603, was probably written within the previous eighteen months. This entry did not result in publication; the play was re-entered on 28 January 1609, and a quarto appeared during that year. The version printed in the 1623 Folio adds a Prologue, and has many variations in dialogue. It includes the epilogue spoken by [[characters/pandarus|Pandarus]] (which we print as an Additional Passage), but certain features of the text suggest that it does so by accident, and that the epilogue had been marked for omission. Our text is based in substance on the Folio in the belief that this represents the play in its later, revised form.
The story of the siege of Troy was the main subject of one of the greatest surviving works of classical literature, Homer’s Iliad, probably Shakespeare read George Chapman’s 1598 [[forms/translation|translation]] of Books 1―2 and 7―11. The story also figures prominently in Virgil’s Aeneid and Ovid’s Metamorphoses, both of which Shakespeare knew [[motifs/well|well]]. The war between Greece and Troy had been provoked by the abduction of the Grecian [[characters/helen|Helen]] (better, if confusingly, known as [[characters/helen|Helen]] of Troy) by the Trojan [[archetypes/hero|hero]] [[settings/paris|Paris]], son of [[archetypes/king|King]] Priam. Shakespeare’s play opens when the Greek forces, led by Menelaus’ brother [[characters/agamemnon|Agamemnon]], have already been besieging Troy for seven years. Shakespeare concentrates on the opposition between the Greek [[archetypes/hero|hero]] [[characters/achilles|Achilles]] and the Trojan [[characters/hector|Hector]]. In the Folio, [[characters/troilus|Troilus]] and [[characters/cressida|Cressida]] is printed among the tragedies; if there is a [[archetypes/tragic-hero|tragic hero]], it is [[characters/hector|Hector]].
Shakespeare also shows how the war caused by one [[motifs/love|love]] affair destroys another. The stories of the [[motifs/love|love]] between the Trojan [[characters/troilus|Troilus]] and the Grecian [[characters/cressida|Cressida]], encouraged by her uncle [[characters/pandarus|Pandarus]], and of [[characters/cressida|Cressida]]’s desertion of [[characters/troilus|Troilus]] for the Greek Diomedes, are medieval additions to the heroic narrative. Chaucer’s long poem [[characters/troilus|Troilus]] and Criseyde was already a classic, and Shakespeare would also have known Robert Henryson’s continuation, The Testament of Cresseid, in which [[characters/cressida|Cressida]], deserted by Diomedes, dwindles into a leprous beggar.
[[characters/troilus|Troilus]] and [[characters/cressida|Cressida]] is a demanding play, Shakespeare’s third longest, highly philosophical in tone and with an exceptionally learned vocabulary. Possibly (as has often been conjectured) he wrote it for private performance; the 1603 Stationers’ Register entry says it had been acted by the [[archetypes/king|King]]’s Men, and the original title-page of the 1609 quarto repeats this claim, but while the edition was being printed this title-page was replaced by one that does not mention performance, and an epistle was added claiming that it was ‘a new play, never staled with the stage, never clapper-clawed with the palms of the vulgar’. An adaptation by John Dryden of 1679 was successfully acted from [[motifs/time|time]] to [[motifs/time|time]] for half a century, but the first verified performance of Shakespeare’s play was in [[settings/germany|Germany]] in 1898, and that was heavily adapted. [[characters/troilus|Troilus]] and [[characters/cressida|Cressida]] came into its own in the twentieth century, when its deflation of heroes, its radical questioning of human values (especially in relation to [[motifs/love|love]] and war), and its remorseless examination of the frailty of human aspirations in the face of the destructive powers of [[motifs/time|time]] seemed particularly apposite to modern intellectual and ethical preoccupations.
THE PERSONS OF THE PLAY
PROLOGUE
Trojans
PRIAM, [[archetypes/king|King]] of Troy
CASSANDRA, Priam’s daughter, a prophetess
ANDROMACHE, wife of [[characters/hector|Hector]]
[[characters/pandarus|PANDARUS]], a lord
[[characters/cressida|CRESSIDA]], his niece
CALCHAS, her father, who has joined the Greeks
[[characters/helen|HELEN]], wife of Menelaus, now living with [[settings/paris|Paris]]
ALEXANDER, servant of [[characters/cressida|Cressida]]
Servants of [[characters/troilus|Troilus]], musicians, soldiers, attendants
Greeks
[[characters/agamemnon|AGAMEMNON]], Commander-in-Chief
MENELAUS, his brother
NESTOR
[[characters/ulysses|ULYSSES]]
[[characters/achilles|ACHILLES]]
PATROCLUS, his companion
DIOMEDES
[[characters/ajax|AJAX]]
[[characters/thersites|THERSITES]]
MYRMIDONS, soldiers of [[characters/achilles|Achilles]]
Servants of Diomedes, soldiers

[[characters/troilus|Troilus]] and [[characters/cressida|Cressida]]

Prologue
Enter the Prologue armed
PROLOGUE
In Troy there lies the scene. From isles of Greece
The princes orgulous, their high [[motifs/blood|blood]] chafed,
Have to the port of Athens sent their ships,
Fraught with the ministers and instruments
Of cruel war. Sixty-and-nine, that wore
Their crownets regal, from th‘Athenian bay
Put forth toward Phrygia, and their vow is made
To ransack Troy, within whose strong immures
The ravished [[characters/helen|Helen]], Menelaus’ [[characters/queen|queen]],
With wanton [[settings/paris|Paris]] steeps—and that’s the quarrel.
To Tenedos they come,
And the deep-drawing barques do there disgorge
Their warlike freightage; now on Dardan plains
The fresh and yet unbruisèd Greeks do pitch
Their brave pavilions. Priam’s six-gated [[motifs/city|city]]—
Dardan and Timbria, Helias, Chetas, Troien,
And Antenorides—with massy staples
And corresponsive and full-filling bolts
Spar up the sons of Troy.
Now expectation, tickling skittish spirits
On one and other side, Trojan and Greek,
Sets all on hazard. And hither am I come,
A Prologue armed—but not in confidence
Of author’s pen or actor’s voice, but suited
In like conditions as our argument—
To tell you, fair beholders, that our play
Leaps o’er the vaunt and firstlings of those broils,
Beginning in the middle, starting thence away
To what may be digested in a play.
Like or find fault; do as your pleasures are;
Now, good or bad, ’tis but the chance of war.
Exit

### Atto 1, Scena 1

| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Entrano [[characters/pandarus\|Pandaro]], e [[characters/troilus\|Troilo]] in armi |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILO]] | Chiamate qui il mio scudiero. Voglio disarmarmi ancora.<br>Perché dovrei guerreggiare fuori dalle mura di Troia,<br>io che trovo qui dentro battaglia sì crudele?<br>Ogni Troiano che sia padrone del proprio cuore,<br>vada a Betd—Troitus, ahimè, non ne ha alcuno. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARO]] | Non si porrà mai rimedio a questa faccenda? |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILO]] | I Greci sono forti, e alla loro forza pari è l’arte,<br>feroci all’arte loro, e alla ferocia valorosi.<br>Ma io son più debole della lacrima d’una donna,<br>più mansueto del [[motifs/sleep\|sonno]], più sciocco dell’ignoranza,<br>meno valoroso della vergine nella [[motifs/night\|notte]],<br>e inesperto come infanzia senza pratica. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARO]] | [[motifs/well\|Bene]], di questo ve n’ho detto abbastanza. Per parte mia, non m’impiccerò né m’intrometterò più oltre. Chi vuole la focaccia dal grano deve aspettare la macinatura. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILO]] | Non ho aspettato? |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARO]] | Sì, la macinatura; ma dovete aspettare la setacciatura. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILO]] | Non ho aspettato? |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARO]] | Sì, la setacciatura; ma dovete aspettare la lievitazione. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILO]] | Anche questo ho aspettato. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARO]] | Sì, fino alla lievitazione; ma nella parola ‘poi’ restano ancora l’impastare, il fare la focaccia, lo scaldare il forno, e la cottura—anzi, dovete attendere anche il raffreddamento, o vi potrà accadere di scottarvi le labbra. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILO]] | La Pazienza stessa, qual dea ella sia,<br>meno di me trasalisce nel patire.<br>Alla regal mensa di Priamo io siedo,<br>e quando la bella Cressida mi viene in pensiero—<br>Ah, traditore! ‘Quando viene’? E quando mai ne parte? |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARO]] | [[motifs/well\|Bene]], iersera appariva più bella di quanto io l’abbia mai vista, o qualunque altra donna. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILO]] | Stavo per dirti: quando il mio cuore,<br>come spaccato da un sospiro, voleva fendersi in due,<br>per timore che [[characters/hector\|Ettore]] o mio padre s’accorgessero di me,<br>io, come quando il sole illumina di traverso,<br>ho sepolto quel sospiro nella piega d’un sorriso.<br>Ma il dolore che sta celato in finta allegria<br>è come quel riso che il fato volge in subita tristezza. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARO]] | Se i suoi capelli non fossero un poco più scuri di quelli d’[[characters/helen\|Elena]]—[[motifs/well\|bene]], via, non ci sarebbe più confronto fra le due donne. Ma, per parte mia, ella è mia parente; non vorrei, come dicono, ‘lodarla’. Però avrei voluto che qualcuno l’avesse sentita parlare ieri, come l’ho sentita io. Non voglio sparlare dell’ingegno di vostra sorella Cassandra, ma— |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILO]] | O [[characters/pandarus\|Pandaro]]! Io ti dico, [[characters/pandarus\|Pandaro]],<br>quando ti dico ‘Là le mie speranze giacciono annegate’,<br>non replicare a quante braccia di profondità<br>stiano sommerse. Ti dico che son pazzo<br>d’[[motifs/love\|amore]] per Cressida; tu rispondi ‘È bella’,<br>e versi nell’aperta piaga del mio cuore<br>i suoi occhi, i suoi capelli, la guancia, l’andatura, la voce;<br>maneggi nel tuo discorso, oh, quella sua mano,<br>al cui paragone ogni biancore è inchiostro<br>che scrive il proprio biasimo, al cui morbido tocco<br>la piuma del cigno è ruvida, e il più fine spirito del senso<br>duro come il palmo dell’aratore. Questo tu mi dici—<br>e con verità me lo dici—quando io dico che l’[[motifs/love\|amo]].<br>Ma dicendo così, invece d’olio e di balsamo<br>tu poni in ogni squarcio che l’[[motifs/love\|amore]] m’ha inflitto<br>il coltello che l’ha aperto. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARO]] | Non dico più che la [[concepts/truth\|verità]]. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILO]] | Non ne dici tanta. |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARO]] | In [[concepts/faith\|fede]] mia, non voglio impicciarmene. Sia com’è. Se è bella, tanto meglio per lei; se non lo è, ha in mano il rimedio da sé. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILO]] | Buon [[characters/pandarus\|Pandaro]], suvvia, [[characters/pandarus\|Pandaro]]! |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARO]] | Ho avuto per compenso la mia fatica. Male giudicato da lei e male giudicato da voi. Andato avanti e indietro a far da mezzano, e poche grazie per il mio travaglio. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILO]] | Come, sei in collera, [[characters/pandarus\|Pandaro]]? Come, con me? |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARO]] | Perché è mia parente, dunque non è bella come [[characters/helen\|Elena]]. Se non fosse mia parente, sarebbe bella il venerdì quanto [[characters/helen\|Elena]] lo è la [[characters/sunday\|domenica]]. Ma che m’importa? Non m’importerebbe s’ella fosse una mora d’Africa. Per me è tutt’uno. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILO]] | Ho detto io che non è bella? |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARO]] | Non m’importa che l’abbiate detto o no. È una [[archetypes/fool\|sciocca]] a restare indietro, lontana dal padre. Vada dai Greci—e così le dirò la prossima [[motifs/time\|volta]] che la vedrò. Per parte mia, non m’impiccerò né m’intrometterò più nella faccenda. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILO]] | [[characters/pandarus\|Pandaro]]— |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARO]] | Io no. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILO]] | Dolce [[characters/pandarus\|Pandaro]]— |
| [[characters/pandarus\|PANDARO]] | Vi prego, non parlatemi più. Lascerò tutto come l’ho trovato. E qui finisca. |
| *(didascalia)* | Esce |
| *(didascalia)* | Squillo d’allarme |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILO]] | Pace, clamori sgraziati! Pace, suoni rozzi!<br>Folli da entrambe le parti. [[characters/helen\|Elena]] dev’essere bella per forza,<br>se col vostro [[motifs/blood\|sangue]] la dipingete così ogni giorno.<br>Io non posso combattere per questa causa.<br>È soggetto troppo scarno per la mia spada.<br>Ma [[characters/pandarus\|Pandaro]]—o dèi, come mi tormentate!<br>Non posso giungere a Cressida se non per mezzo di Pandaro,<br>ed egli è tanto ritroso a farsi pregare di pregare<br>quanto ella è ostinatamente casta contro ogni corteggiamento.<br>Dimmi, [[characters/apollo\|Apollo]], per l’[[motifs/love\|amore]] della tua Dafne,<br>che cosa è Cressida, che cosa Pandaro, e che cosa noi?<br>Il suo letto è l’India; là ella giace, una perla.<br>Fra il nostro Ilio e il luogo ov’ella dimora<br>sia detto il flutto selvaggio ed errabondo,<br>noi stessi il mercante, e questo Pandaro nocchiero<br>la nostra dubbia [[concepts/hope\|speranza]], la nostra scorta, e la nostra barca. |
| *(didascalia)* | Squillo d’allarme. Entra Enea |
| ENEA | Ebbene, principe [[characters/troilus\|Troilo]]? Perché non in campo? |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILO]] | Perché non vi sono. Questa risposta da donna s’addice,<br>ché femmineo è l’esserne lontano.<br>Che nuove, Enea, dal campo oggi? |
| ENEA | Che [[settings/paris\|Paride]] è tornato a [[motifs/home\|casa]], e ferito. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILO]] | Da chi, Enea? |
| ENEA | [[characters/troilus\|Troilo]], da Menelao. |
| [[characters/troilus\|TROILO]] | Lasciate che [[settings/paris\|Paride]] sanguini, non è che una cicatrice da schernire:<br>[[settings/paris\|Paride]] è stato incornato dal corno di Menelao. |
